\begin{warningbox}[Priority and regularity discipline]
A theorem labelled ``proved here'' is supplied with a proof in this document,
but has not necessarily been formalized in Lean.  Novelty is relative to the
pinned repository corpus.  Standard constructions from fractional calculus
are cited rather than claimed as new; the new content is their exact
specialization and interaction with the Fabius--Rvachev structure.  Whenever
a Caputo derivative requires endpoint regularity that the inverse $Q$ may not
possess at higher order, the hypothesis is stated explicitly.
\end{warningbox}

\section{Normalization and structural input}
\label{p9:sec:normalization}

\subsection{The bounded function, inverse, and compact density}

Following the probabilistic construction of Fabius and the compact-support
formulation of Rvachev/Arias de Reyna \cite{p9:Fabius1966,p9:Arias1982,p9:Arias2017},
let $U_1,U_2,\ldots$ be independent uniform random variables on $[0,1]$ and
set
\begin{equation}
  Y=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-j}U_j,
  \qquad
  Z=2Y-1=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-j}V_j,
  \label{p9:eq:random-series}
\end{equation}
where $V_j=2U_j-1$ is uniform on $[-1,1]$.  The bounded Fabius function is the
CDF of $Y$:
\begin{equation}
 F(x)=\Prob(Y\le x),
 \qquad
 F(x)=0\ (x\le0),
 \qquad
 F(x)=1\ (x\ge1).
 \label{p9:eq:F-cdf}
\end{equation}
The variable $Z$ has the even density $\Up$ supported on $[-1,1]$.  Thus
\begin{align}
 F'(x)&=2\Up(2x-1), &&0<x<1,\label{p9:eq:Fprime-up}\\
 \Up(t)&=F(1-|t|), &&|t|\le1,\label{p9:eq:up-F}\\
 F(1-x)&=1-F(x),& Q(1-y)&=1-Q(y).\label{p9:eq:symmetry}
\end{align}
On $[0,1]$, $\Up(x)=1-F(x)=F(1-x)$.  The inverse
$Q=F^{-1}$ is continuous on $[0,1]$, smooth on $(0,1)$, and satisfies
\begin{equation}
 Q'(y)=\frac1{F'(Q(y))}
      =\frac1{2\Up(2Q(y)-1)}.
 \label{p9:eq:Qprime}
\end{equation}

The left-half self-differential equation is
\begin{equation}
 F'(x)=2F(2x),\qquad 0\le x\le\frac12,
 \label{p9:eq:F-selfdiff}
\end{equation}
and Rvachev's global differential--difference equation is
\begin{equation}
 \Up'(x)=2\bigl(\Up(2x+1)-\Up(2x-1)\bigr).
 \label{p9:eq:up-dde}
\end{equation}
Every derivative of $F$ vanishes at $0$ and $1$, and every derivative of
$\Up$ vanishes at $\pm1$.

\subsection{Fourier products and moments}

We use the angular-frequency convention
\begin{equation}
 \widehat f(\xi)=\int_{\R}f(x)\e^{-\ii\xi x}\dd x,
 \qquad
 f(x)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{\R}\widehat f(\xi)\e^{\ii\xi x}\dd\xi.
 \label{p9:eq:Fourier-convention}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
 \phi(\xi):=\widehat\Up(\xi)
 =\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\sincPartcc\!\left(\frac{\xi}{2^j}\right),
 \qquad \sincPartcc z=\frac{\sin z}{z},
 \label{p9:eq:sinc-product}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
 \phi(\xi)=\sincPartcc(\xi/2)\phi(\xi/2).
 \label{p9:eq:phi-refine}
\end{equation}
The one-sided moment generating function is
\begin{equation}
 P(s)=\E\e^{sY}
 =\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{\e^{s/2^j}-1}{s/2^j},
 \qquad
 P(2s)=\frac{\e^s-1}{s}P(s).
 \label{p9:eq:mgf-product}
\end{equation}
Write
\begin{equation}
 m_n=\E Y^n,
 \qquad
 c_k=\E Z^{2k}=\int_{-1}^1t^{2k}\Up(t)\dd t.
 \label{p9:eq:moments}
\end{equation}
All these moments are rational.  The first centered values are
\begin{equation}
 c_0=1,
 \qquad c_1=\frac19,
 \qquad c_2=\frac{19}{675},
 \qquad c_3=\frac{583}{59535}.
 \label{p9:eq:first-c}
\end{equation}
The recurrence
\begin{equation}
 (2^n-1)m_n
 =\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\binom nj\frac{m_j}{n-j+1}
 \label{p9:eq:moment-rec}
\end{equation}
will be used for numerical coefficients.

\subsection{Endpoint rapid variation}

The repository's Lambert--$W$ analysis implies, in particular, that $F$ is
rapidly varying at $0$:
\begin{equation}
 \frac{F(tx)}{F(x)}\longrightarrow0
 \qquad(x\downarrow0)
 \quad\text{for every fixed }0\le t<1.
 \label{p9:eq:rapid-variation}
\end{equation}
For $x\le1/2$, $F$ is convex because differentiating
\cref{p9:eq:F-selfdiff} gives $F''(x)=4F'(2x)\ge0$.  Consequently
\begin{equation}
 F(tx)\le tF(x),
 \qquad 0\le t\le1,
 \quad 0\le x\le\frac12.
 \label{p9:eq:convex-bound}
\end{equation}
The combination of \cref{p9:eq:rapid-variation,p9:eq:convex-bound} is the key to the
fractional inverse endpoint limits in \cref{p9:sec:endpoint-fractional}.

\section{Existing ordinary and Volterra calculus: a compact atlas}
\label{p9:sec:baseline-atlas}

This section fixes notation and records the strongest formulas already present
in the current corpus.  They are indispensable inputs, but they are not
novelty claims of this report.

\subsection{Integer derivative and primitive orbits}

Let $\Fext$ be the signed global extension satisfying
$\Fext'(x)=2\Fext(2x)$ and agreeing with $F$ on $(-\infty,1]$.  For
$n\in\N_0$,
\begin{equation}
 D^n\Fext(x)=2^{\binom{n+1}{2}}\Fext(2^nx).
 \label{p9:eq:integer-derivative-orbit}
\end{equation}
For the normalized zero-based Volterra operator
\begin{equation}
 (J^nf)(x)=
 \begin{cases}
 f(x),&n=0,\\[1mm]
 \displaystyle\frac1{(n-1)!}\int_0^x(x-t)^{n-1}f(t)\dd t,&n\ge1,
 \end{cases}
 \label{p9:eq:J-def}
\end{equation}
its exact inverse orbit is
\begin{equation}
 J^n\Fext(x)=2^{\binom n2}\Fext\!\left(\frac{x}{2^n}\right).
 \label{p9:eq:integer-primitive-orbit}
\end{equation}
The same formula holds for bounded $F$ whenever the entire integration segment
lies in the agreement range; in particular, for $0\le x\le1$.
Every other $n$-fold antiderivative differs from \cref{p9:eq:integer-primitive-orbit}
by a polynomial of degree at most $n-1$.

\subsection{Monomially weighted primitives}

For $x>0$, $n\ge1$, and complex $a$, endpoint flatness makes the following
Newton--Volterra series locally normally convergent in $(a,x)$:
\begin{equation}
 \frac1{(n-1)!}\int_0^x(x-t)^{n-1}t^aF(t)\dd t
 =\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}(-1)^r
 \binom{n+r-1}{r}\fall{a}{r}
 x^{a-r}2^{\binom{n+r}{2}}
 F\!\left(\frac{x}{2^{n+r}}\right).
 \label{p9:eq:weighted-newton-volterra}
\end{equation}
For $a\in\N_0$ it terminates and is a finite native closed form.  Negative and
fractional powers are covered by the same convergent series.  On $[0,1]$,
weights multiplying $1-F=\Up$ follow by subtracting
$J^n(t^aF(t))$ from the elementary Volterra integral of $t^a$.

\subsection{Inverse primitives and ordinary derivatives}

For $q=Q(y)$ and $\Re p>0$,
\begin{align}
 \int_0^yQ(v)^p\dd v
 &=yq^p-p\int_0^qx^{p-1}F(x)\dd x,
 \label{p9:eq:Q-power-primitive}\\
 \int_0^yQ(v)\dd v
 &=yQ(y)-F\!\left(\frac{Q(y)}2\right).
 \label{p9:eq:Q-first-primitive}
\end{align}
The $n$-fold inverse primitive has the incomplete-moment form
\begin{equation}
 I_{0+}^nQ^p(y)
 =\frac{p}{n!}\int_0^{Q(y)}x^{p-1}(y-F(x))^n\dd x.
 \label{p9:eq:Q-n-primitive}
\end{equation}
For ordinary derivatives, introduce
\begin{equation}
 L_F=\frac1{F'(x)}\frac{\dd}{\dd x}.
 \label{p9:eq:LF}
\end{equation}
Then the exact inverse-chain identity is
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd^n}{\dd y^n}H(Q(y))
 =\bigl(L_F^nH\bigr)(Q(y)).
 \label{p9:eq:inverse-chain}
\end{equation}
In particular, $Q^{(n)}=(L_F^n\operatorname{id})\circ Q$.  Equivalently,
differentiating $F(Q(y))=y$ gives the Bell-polynomial recurrence
\begin{equation}
 Q^{(n)}(y)
 =-\frac1{F'(Q(y))}
 \sum_{k=2}^nF^{(k)}(Q(y))
 B_{n,k}\bigl(Q'(y),\ldots,Q^{(n-k+1)}(y)\bigr).
 \label{p9:eq:Q-Bell}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Standard Riemann--Liouville primitives}

For $\Re\alpha>0$, using the standard Riemann--Liouville convention
\cite{p9:Samko,p9:Podlubny},
\begin{equation}
 (I_{0+}^\alpha f)(x)
 =\frac1{\Gamma(\alpha)}\int_0^x(x-t)^{\alpha-1}f(t)\dd t.
 \label{p9:eq:RL-def}
\end{equation}
The corpus already proves the probabilistic formulas
\begin{align}
 I_{0+}^\alpha F(x)
 &=\frac{\E[(x-Y)_+^\alpha]}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)},
 \label{p9:eq:RL-F-prob}\\
 I_{-1+}^\alpha\Up(x)
 &=\frac{\E[(x-Z)_+^{\alpha-1}]}{\Gamma(\alpha)},
 \label{p9:eq:RL-up-prob}\\
 I_{0+}^\alpha Q^p(y)
 &=\frac{p}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}
   \int_0^{Q(y)}x^{p-1}(y-F(x))^\alpha\dd x.
 \label{p9:eq:RL-Qp-baseline}
\end{align}
For $\Re\alpha>1$,
\begin{equation}
 I_{0+}^\alpha F(x)
 =2^{\alpha-1}I_{0+}^{\alpha-1}F(x/2),
 \label{p9:eq:RL-dyadic-recurrence}
\end{equation}
which interpolates the integer primitive ladder but does not collapse a
standard noninteger derivative to a single dilated Fabius value.

\section{Exact fractional calculus in the Fabius and inverse clocks}
\label{p9:sec:F-clock}

Standard fractional calculus measures memory in the Euclidean difference
$x-t$.  The Fabius function supplies a second natural clock: probability mass
$F(x)-F(t)$.  Fractional calculus with respect to a function is classical in
general form \cite{p9:Oumarou2021}; the exact Fabius specialization below appears
to be absent from the audited corpus.

\subsection{Conjugacy definition}

Define the composition operator
\begin{equation}
 (\mathcal C_Fh)(y)=h(Q(y)),
 \qquad
 (\mathcal C_F^{-1}g)(x)=g(F(x)).
 \label{p9:eq:CF}
\end{equation}

\begin{definition}[Fabius-clock integral and derivative]
For $\Re\alpha>0$,
\begin{equation}
 I_F^\alpha=\mathcal C_F^{-1}I_{0+}^\alpha\mathcal C_F.
 \label{p9:eq:IF-conjugacy}
\end{equation}
The first-order Fabius-clock derivative is
\begin{equation}
 D_F=\mathcal C_F^{-1}D\mathcal C_F
 =\frac1{F'}\frac{\dd}{\dd x}.
 \label{p9:eq:DF-conjugacy}
\end{equation}
For $n-1<\Re\beta<n$, transport the usual Riemann--Liouville and Caputo
operators \cite{p9:Caputo1967,p9:Podlubny} by defining
\begin{align}
 {}^{\mathrm{RL}}D_F^\beta
 &=D_F^nI_F^{n-\beta},
 \label{p9:eq:RL-DF}\\
 {}^{\Ccap}D_F^\beta
 &=I_F^{n-\beta}D_F^n.
 \label{p9:eq:Caputo-DF}
\end{align}
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[kernel, semigroup, and exact conjugacy]
For suitable $h$,
\begin{equation}
 (I_F^\alpha h)(x)
 =\frac1{\Gamma(\alpha)}
 \int_0^x(F(x)-F(t))^{\alpha-1}h(t)F'(t)\dd t.
 \label{p9:eq:IF-kernel}
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
 I_F^\alpha I_F^\gamma=I_F^{\alpha+\gamma},
 \qquad
 {}^{\Ccap}D_F^\beta
 =\mathcal C_F^{-1}{}^{\Ccap}D_{0+}^\beta\mathcal C_F,
 \qquad
 {}^{\mathrm{RL}}D_F^\beta
 =\mathcal C_F^{-1}D_{0+}^\beta\mathcal C_F.
 \label{p9:eq:F-clock-conjugacies}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Evaluate \cref{p9:eq:IF-conjugacy} at $y=F(x)$ and substitute $v=F(t)$:
\[
 \frac1{\Gamma(\alpha)}\int_0^{F(x)}
 (F(x)-v)^{\alpha-1}h(Q(v))\dd v
 =\frac1{\Gamma(\alpha)}\int_0^x
 (F(x)-F(t))^{\alpha-1}h(t)F'(t)\dd t.
\]
Every operator identity is then transported from ordinary fractional calculus
by conjugacy.  In particular, the semigroup is literal rather than formal.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Power laws and Mittag--Leffler eigenfunctions}

\begin{theorem}[diagonal action on powers]
For $\Re\lambda>-1$ and $\Re\alpha>0$,
\begin{equation}
 I_F^\alpha F(x)^\lambda
 =\frac{\Gamma(\lambda+1)}{\Gamma(\lambda+\alpha+1)}
  F(x)^{\lambda+\alpha}.
 \label{p9:eq:F-clock-power-integral}
\end{equation}
For the Riemann--Liouville derivative, wherever both sides are defined,
\begin{equation}
 {}^{\mathrm{RL}}D_F^\beta F(x)^\lambda
 =\frac{\Gamma(\lambda+1)}{\Gamma(\lambda-\beta+1)}
  F(x)^{\lambda-\beta}.
 \label{p9:eq:F-clock-power-RL}
\end{equation}
For $n-1<\beta<n$, the same formula holds for the Caputo derivative when
$\Re\lambda>n-1$; the usual low-degree polynomial exceptions apply.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Under $\mathcal C_F$, the function $F(x)^\lambda$ becomes $y^\lambda$.
The assertions are exactly the beta-integral power laws for ordinary
Riemann--Liouville and Caputo operators.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[fractional eigenproblem]
For $0<\beta\le1$ and $a\in\C$,
\begin{equation}
 {}^{\Ccap}D_F^\beta
 E_\beta\bigl(aF(x)^\beta\bigr)
 =aE_\beta\bigl(aF(x)^\beta\bigr),
 \qquad
 E_\beta(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^k}{\Gamma(\beta k+1)}.
 \label{p9:eq:F-clock-Mittag}
\end{equation}
Thus the ordinary exponential $\e^{aF(x)}$ is recovered at $\beta=1$.
\end{corollary}

\subsection{The dual inverse clock}

The inverse function supplies the dual clock on the probability interval.
Define
\begin{equation}
 (I_Q^\alpha k)(y)
 =\frac1{\Gamma(\alpha)}
  \int_0^y(Q(y)-Q(v))^{\alpha-1}k(v)Q'(v)\dd v,
 \qquad
 D_Q=\frac1{Q'}\frac{\dd}{\dd y}.
 \label{p9:eq:IQ-DQ}
\end{equation}
This is conjugate to ordinary fractional calculus by $x=Q(y)$.  Hence
\begin{align}
 I_Q^\alpha Q(y)^\lambda
 &=\frac{\Gamma(\lambda+1)}{\Gamma(\lambda+\alpha+1)}
 Q(y)^{\lambda+\alpha},
 \label{p9:eq:Q-clock-power-I}\\
 {}^{\mathrm{RL}}D_Q^\beta Q(y)^\lambda
 &=\frac{\Gamma(\lambda+1)}{\Gamma(\lambda-\beta+1)}
 Q(y)^{\lambda-\beta}.
 \label{p9:eq:Q-clock-power-D}
\end{align}
The pair $(I_F,D_F)$ and $(I_Q,D_Q)$ is therefore exactly dual under the
mutually inverse coordinate maps $F$ and $Q$.

\begin{remark}[What this does and does not say]
The Fabius-clock derivative is not the standard fractional derivative of
$F$ as a function of Euclidean $x$.  It is a nonlinear-coordinate operator in
which the intricate Fabius geometry is moved into the kernel.  Its advantage
is exact algebra; the standard Caputo and Weyl derivatives studied below
retain the original Euclidean clock and are analytically more delicate.
\end{remark}

\section{Caputo and Riemann--Liouville transmutation through the inverse}
\label{p9:sec:inverse-transmutation}

The ordinary inverse-chain rule \cref{p9:eq:inverse-chain} has a nonlocal
fractional counterpart.  It converts a singular derivative of $H\circ Q$ in
the $y$ variable into an integral over the much better behaved $x$ variable.

\subsection{Left fractional integrals and derivatives}

\begin{theorem}[general inverse-composition transmutation]
\label{p9:thm:left-transmutation}
Let $q=Q(y)$ and $\Re\alpha>0$.  If $H$ is integrable against the displayed
kernels, then
\begin{align}
 I_{0+}^\alpha[H\circ Q](y)
 &=\frac1{\Gamma(\alpha)}
   \int_0^q(y-F(x))^{\alpha-1}H(x)F'(x)\dd x,
 \label{p9:eq:inverse-RL-substitution}\\
 &=\frac{H(0)y^\alpha}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}
   +\frac1{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}
    \int_0^q(y-F(x))^\alpha H'(x)\dd x,
 \label{p9:eq:inverse-RL-parts}
\end{align}
where the second identity assumes absolute continuity of $H$.
For $0<\beta<1$,
\begin{equation}
 {}^{\Ccap}D_{0+}^\beta[H\circ Q](y)
 =\frac1{\Gamma(1-\beta)}
  \int_0^q(y-F(x))^{-\beta}H'(x)\dd x,
 \label{p9:eq:inverse-Caputo-transmutation}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
 D_{0+}^\beta[H\circ Q](y)
 =\frac{H(0)y^{-\beta}}{\Gamma(1-\beta)}
  +{}^{\Ccap}D_{0+}^\beta[H\circ Q](y).
 \label{p9:eq:inverse-RL-vs-Caputo}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Substitute $v=F(x)$ in the definition of $I_{0+}^\alpha[H(Q(v))]$ to obtain
\cref{p9:eq:inverse-RL-substitution}.  Since
\[
 \dd (y-F(x))^\alpha
 =-\alpha(y-F(x))^{\alpha-1}F'(x)\dd x,
\]
integration by parts gives \cref{p9:eq:inverse-RL-parts}.
For the Caputo derivative,
\[
 \frac{\dd}{\dd v}H(Q(v))=H'(Q(v))Q'(v).
\]
After $v=F(x)$, the factors $Q'(F(x))F'(x)$ cancel exactly, leaving
\cref{p9:eq:inverse-Caputo-transmutation}.  The final identity is the standard
Riemann--Liouville/Caputo endpoint correction.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[powers of the inverse Fabius function]
For $r>0$ and $0<\beta<1$,
\begin{equation}
 {}^{\Ccap}D_{0+}^\beta Q(y)^r
 =\frac{r}{\Gamma(1-\beta)}
   \int_0^{Q(y)}x^{r-1}(y-F(x))^{-\beta}\dd x.
 \label{p9:eq:Caputo-Q-power}
\end{equation}
Because $Q(0)=0$, the Riemann--Liouville and Caputo derivatives coincide for
$Q^r$ in this range whenever the integral is finite.
\end{corollary}

\subsection{All positive noninteger orders}

Let $n-1<\beta<n$.  From \cref{p9:eq:inverse-chain},
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd^n}{\dd y^n}[H(Q(y))]
 =\bigl(L_F^nH\bigr)(Q(y)).
\end{equation}
Therefore:

\begin{proposition}[higher-order Caputo transmutation]
Suppose $H\circ Q$ belongs to the classical Caputo domain on $[0,y]$ and
$(L_F^nH)(x)F'(x)$ is integrable against the displayed kernel.  Then
\begin{equation}
 {}^{\Ccap}D_{0+}^\beta[H\circ Q](y)
 =\frac1{\Gamma(n-\beta)}
  \int_0^{Q(y)}(y-F(x))^{n-\beta-1}
  (L_F^nH)(x)F'(x)\dd x.
 \label{p9:eq:higher-Caputo-transmutation}
\end{equation}
The Riemann--Liouville derivative is obtained by adding the usual finite
endpoint jet
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n-1}
 \frac{(H\circ Q)^{(k)}(0+)}{\Gamma(k-\beta+1)}y^{k-\beta},
 \label{p9:eq:higher-RL-jet}
\end{equation}
when these endpoint values exist.
\end{proposition}

\begin{warningbox}[Higher-order inverse derivatives]
The inverse Fabius function has singular derivatives at $0$ and $1$.
Consequently, a formula obtained by formally inserting $H(x)=x$ into
\cref{p9:eq:higher-Caputo-transmutation} need not define a classical Caputo
derivative for every $\beta>1$.  The theorem is deliberately stated in terms
of the actual Caputo domain.  Distributional or weighted-space extensions are
promising, but require a separate endpoint-domain analysis.
\end{warningbox}

\subsection{Right-sided transmutation}

For $0<\beta<1$ and $q=Q(y)$, the right-sided counterparts are
\begin{align}
 I_{1-}^\alpha[H\circ Q](y)
 &=\frac{H(1)(1-y)^\alpha}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}
   -\frac1{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}
    \int_q^1(F(x)-y)^\alpha H'(x)\dd x,
 \label{p9:eq:right-RL-transmutation}\\
 {}^{\Ccap}D_{1-}^\beta[H\circ Q](y)
 &=-\frac1{\Gamma(1-\beta)}
   \int_q^1(F(x)-y)^{-\beta}H'(x)\dd x,
 \label{p9:eq:right-Caputo-transmutation}\\
 D_{1-}^\beta[H\circ Q](y)
 &=\frac{H(1)(1-y)^{-\beta}}{\Gamma(1-\beta)}
   +{}^{\Ccap}D_{1-}^\beta[H\circ Q](y).
 \label{p9:eq:right-RL-Caputo}
\end{align}
They follow by the same substitution and integration by parts.  Symmetry
$Q(1-y)=1-Q(y)$ converts left endpoint statements into right endpoint
statements without recomputing the Fabius asymptotics.

\section{Fractional endpoint laws for inverse powers}
\label{p9:sec:endpoint-fractional}

The integral formulas above permit endpoint asymptotics without differentiating
the Lambert--$W$ inverse expansion term by term.

\begin{theorem}[fractional primitive and Caputo endpoint laws]
\label{p9:thm:Q-fractional-endpoint}
Let $r>0$ and $\alpha>0$.  Then
\begin{equation}
 I_{0+}^\alpha Q(y)^r
 \sim\frac{Q(y)^r y^\alpha}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}
 \qquad(y\downarrow0).
 \label{p9:eq:Q-RL-endpoint}
\end{equation}
For $0<\beta<1$,
\begin{equation}
 {}^{\Ccap}D_{0+}^\beta Q(y)^r
 \sim\frac{Q(y)^r y^{-\beta}}{\Gamma(1-\beta)}
 \qquad(y\downarrow0).
 \label{p9:eq:Q-Caputo-endpoint}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put $q=Q(y)$ and scale $x=qt$ in
\cref{p9:eq:RL-Qp-baseline}.  The ratio of the two sides of
\cref{p9:eq:Q-RL-endpoint} is
\begin{equation}
 r\int_0^1t^{r-1}
 \left(1-\frac{F(qt)}{F(q)}\right)^\alpha\dd t.
 \label{p9:eq:RL-endpoint-ratio}
\end{equation}
For each fixed $t<1$, rapid variation \cref{p9:eq:rapid-variation} makes the
parenthesis tend to $1$.  The integrand is bounded by $rt^{r-1}$, so dominated
convergence gives $1$.

Likewise, \cref{p9:eq:Caputo-Q-power} yields the ratio
\begin{equation}
 r\int_0^1t^{r-1}
 \left(1-\frac{F(qt)}{F(q)}\right)^{-\beta}\dd t.
 \label{p9:eq:Caputo-endpoint-ratio}
\end{equation}
For $q\le1/2$, convexity gives $F(qt)/F(q)\le t$; hence the integrand is bounded
by $rt^{r-1}(1-t)^{-\beta}$, which is integrable because $r>0$ and
$\beta<1$.  Dominated convergence again gives $1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[right endpoint]
As $y\uparrow1$, the corresponding right-sided laws hold for
$(1-Q(y))^r$ with $1-y$ in place of $y$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{remark}[Slow convergence]
The limit in \cref{p9:eq:Q-Caputo-endpoint} is rigorous but numerically slow.
For $r=1$, $\beta=0.4$, the ratio in
\cref{p9:eq:Caputo-endpoint-ratio} decreases only from approximately $1.1835$ at
$y=10^{-2}$ to $1.1127$ at $y=10^{-7}$.  This is consistent with a first
correction on the Lambert scale $\rho(y)^{-1}$, where
\begin{equation}
 \rho(y)=\sqrt{\frac{2\log(1/y)}{\log2}}.
 \label{p9:eq:rho-y}
\end{equation}
A phase-resolved conjecture is stated in \cref{p9:conj:Caputo-phase}.
\end{remark}

\section{A standard fractional bridge from Fabius to Rvachev}
\label{p9:sec:F-up-bridge}

Because $F$ is flat at $0$, its left Riemann--Liouville and Caputo derivatives
coincide for every positive noninteger order.  Unlike the Fabius-clock
operator, the following theorem concerns the ordinary Euclidean clock.

\begin{theorem}[all-order fractional $F$--$\Up$ bridge]
\label{p9:thm:F-up-bridge}
Let $n-1<\beta<n$ and $0<x<1$.  Then
\begin{equation}
 D_{0+}^\beta F(x)
 ={}^{\Ccap}D_{0+}^\beta F(x)
 =2^\beta
   \left(I_{-1+}^{n-\beta}\Up^{(n-1)}\right)(2x-1).
 \label{p9:eq:F-up-bridge-general}
\end{equation}
In particular, for $0<\beta<1$,
\begin{equation}
 D_{0+}^\beta F(x)
 =2^\beta\left(I_{-1+}^{1-\beta}\Up\right)(2x-1).
 \label{p9:eq:F-up-bridge-simple}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Flatness removes every endpoint correction between the two fractional
derivatives.  Since $F'(t)=2\Up(2t-1)$,
\begin{equation}
 F^{(n)}(t)=2^n\Up^{(n-1)}(2t-1).
\end{equation}
Insert this into
$I_{0+}^{n-\beta}F^{(n)}(x)$ and substitute $s=2t-1$.  The factors are
$2^n$ from differentiation, $2^{-1}$ from $\dd t$, and
$2^{-(n-\beta-1)}$ from the kernel, leaving exactly $2^\beta$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Interpolation endpoints]
For $0<\beta<1$, letting $\beta\downarrow0$ in
\cref{p9:eq:F-up-bridge-simple} gives
\[
 F(x)=\int_{-1}^{2x-1}\Up(t)\dd t,
\]
whereas $\beta\uparrow1$ gives $F'(x)=2\Up(2x-1)$.  Thus the identity is a
literal continuum between the CDF and its density.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Fractional derivatives of \texorpdfstring{$\Up$}{up} from the support boundary}

On the left half of its support, $\Up(x)=F(x+1)$.  Therefore, for
$-1<x<0$,
\begin{equation}
 D_{-1+}^\beta\Up(x)
 =2^\beta
  \left(I_{-1+}^{n-\beta}\Up^{(n-1)}\right)(2x+1),
 \qquad n-1<\beta<n.
 \label{p9:eq:up-left-fractional}
\end{equation}
On the right half, reflection gives
\begin{equation}
 D_{1-}^\beta\Up(x)
 =2^\beta
  \left(I_{-1+}^{n-\beta}\Up^{(n-1)}\right)(1-2x),
 \qquad 0<x<1,
 \label{p9:eq:up-right-fractional}
\end{equation}
with the standard right-Caputo sign convention.  These are boundary-anchored
identities; they should not be confused with the translation-invariant Weyl
operators in the next section.

\subsection{Fractional primitives of \texorpdfstring{$1-F$}{1-F}}

On $[0,1]$, $\Up(x)=1-F(x)$.  Hence
\begin{equation}
 I_{0+}^\alpha\Up(x)
 =\frac{x^\alpha}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}-I_{0+}^\alpha F(x),
 \label{p9:eq:half-up-RL}
\end{equation}
and the probabilistic formula \cref{p9:eq:RL-F-prob} gives
\begin{equation}
 I_{0+}^\alpha\Up(x)
 =\frac{x^\alpha-\E[(x-Y)_+^\alpha]}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}.
 \label{p9:eq:half-up-truncated}
\end{equation}
At $x=1$, symmetry yields
\begin{equation}
 I_{0+}^\alpha F(1)
 =\frac{\Mell(\alpha)}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)},
 \qquad
 \Mell(s)=\E Y^s=\int_0^1Q(y)^s\dd y,
 \label{p9:eq:RL-endpoint-Mellin}
\end{equation}
so endpoint fractional integration is exactly the entire inverse-moment
function already developed in the corpus.

\section{Whole-line Weyl derivatives and complex-order refinement}
\label{p9:sec:Weyl}

Boundary-anchored fractional derivatives break translation invariance.  The
compact support and entire Fourier product of $\Up$ make Weyl calculus the
natural whole-line alternative.

\subsection{Fourier-multiplier definition}

Following the standard Weyl multiplier conventions \cite{p9:Samko}, choose
principal branches and define, initially on Schwartz functions and then by
tempered-distribution continuation,
\begin{align}
 \mathfrak D_+^\sigma f
 &=\Four^{-1}\bigl[(\ii\xi)^\sigma\widehat f(\xi)\bigr],
 \label{p9:eq:Weyl-left}\\
 \mathfrak D_-^\sigma f
 &=\Four^{-1}\bigl[(-\ii\xi)^\sigma\widehat f(\xi)\bigr].
 \label{p9:eq:Weyl-right}
\end{align}
For $\Re\alpha>0$, the left Weyl integral is
\begin{equation}
 (\mathfrak I_+^\alpha f)(x)
 =\frac1{\Gamma(\alpha)}
  \int_{-\infty}^x(x-t)^{\alpha-1}f(t)\dd t,
 \label{p9:eq:Weyl-integral}
\end{equation}
whose multiplier is $(\ii\xi)^{-\alpha}$ in the boundary-value sense.

\subsection{The complex-order hierarchy}

\begin{theorem}[Weyl--Rvachev refinement hierarchy]
\label{p9:thm:Weyl-hierarchy}
Let
\begin{equation}
 W_\sigma=\mathfrak D_+^\sigma\Up.
 \label{p9:eq:W-sigma}
\end{equation}
For every complex $\sigma$ for which the terms are interpreted consistently
as tempered distributions,
\begin{equation}
 W_\sigma(x)=2^\sigma
 \bigl(W_{\sigma-1}(2x+1)-W_{\sigma-1}(2x-1)\bigr).
 \label{p9:eq:Weyl-refinement}
\end{equation}
If $n-1<\beta<n$, this becomes
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak D_+^\beta\Up(x)
 =2^\beta\left[
  (\mathfrak I_+^{n-\beta}\Up^{(n-1)})(2x+1)
 -(\mathfrak I_+^{n-\beta}\Up^{(n-1)})(2x-1)
 \right].
 \label{p9:eq:Weyl-real-order}
\end{equation}
At $\beta=1$ it reduces to Rvachev's differential--difference
formula~\eqref{p9:eq:up-dde}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Apply $\mathfrak D_+^{\sigma-1}$ to
\cref{p9:eq:up-dde}.  Positive dilation obeys
\[
 \mathfrak D_+^\alpha[f(2x+a)]
 =2^\alpha(\mathfrak D_+^\alpha f)(2x+a).
\]
The prefactor $2$ in \cref{p9:eq:up-dde} therefore produces $2^\sigma$.
Equivalently, multiply the Fourier refinement
\cref{p9:eq:phi-refine} by $(\ii\xi)^\sigma$ and invert.  Finally,
$W_{\beta-1}=\mathfrak I_+^{n-\beta}\Up^{(n-1)}$ by equality of Fourier
multipliers.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[right hierarchy and reflection]
Because $\Up$ is even,
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak D_-^\sigma\Up(x)
 =\mathfrak D_+^\sigma\Up(-x).
 \label{p9:eq:Weyl-reflection}
\end{equation}
The corresponding right-sided refinement is obtained by reflecting
\cref{p9:eq:Weyl-refinement}.
\end{corollary}

\subsection{Riesz symmetrization}

For real $0<\beta<2$, define the Riesz fractional Laplacian independently by
\begin{equation}
 \widehat{(-\Delta)^{\beta/2}f}(\xi)
 =|\xi|^\beta\widehat f(\xi).
 \label{p9:eq:Riesz-Fourier}
\end{equation}
When $\cos(\pi\beta/2)\ne0$,
\begin{equation}
 (-\Delta)^{\beta/2}f
 =\frac{\mathfrak D_+^\beta f+\mathfrak D_-^\beta f}
 {2\cos(\pi\beta/2)}.
 \label{p9:eq:Riesz-Weyl}
\end{equation}
At $\beta=1$ the quotient is understood by the Fourier definition (or via the
Hilbert-transform derivative), not by separately evaluating the vanishing
numerator and denominator.

\section{Generalized Stieltjes transforms of complex order}
\label{p9:sec:Stieltjes}

The ordinary Cauchy transform is only the order-$1$ member of a much richer
family.  For real $z>1$ and $\lambda\in\C$, define
\begin{equation}
 S_\lambda(z)
 =\int_{-1}^1\frac{\Up(t)}{(z-t)^\lambda}\dd t
 =\E(z-Z)^{-\lambda},
 \label{p9:eq:S-lambda}
\end{equation}
where the real logarithm fixes the power.  Analytic continuation in $z$ is
available on the slit plane after choosing a branch.

\subsection{Moment, derivative, and dyadic-order hierarchies}

\begin{theorem}[complete generalized Stieltjes hierarchy]
\label{p9:thm:Stieltjes-hierarchy}
For fixed $z>1$, $S_\lambda(z)$ is entire in $\lambda$.  For $|z|>1$,
\begin{equation}
 S_\lambda(z)
 =z^{-\lambda}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}
   \frac{(\lambda)_{2k}}{(2k)!}\frac{c_k}{z^{2k}},
 \label{p9:eq:S-moment-series}
\end{equation}
locally normally in $(\lambda,z)$.  Its $z$ derivatives satisfy
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd^n}{\dd z^n}S_\lambda(z)
 =(-1)^n(\lambda)_nS_{\lambda+n}(z).
 \label{p9:eq:S-derivative-hierarchy}
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
 S_\lambda(z)
 =2^{\lambda-1}\int_{-1}^{1}S_\lambda(2z-v)\dd v,
 \label{p9:eq:S-integral-refinement}
\end{equation}
and, for $\lambda\ne1$,
\begin{equation}
 S_\lambda(z)
 =\frac{2^{\lambda-1}}{1-\lambda}
  \left(S_{\lambda-1}(2z+1)-S_{\lambda-1}(2z-1)\right).
 \label{p9:eq:S-order-lowering}
\end{equation}
At $\lambda=1$, if
\begin{equation}
 L(z)=\E\log(z-Z),
 \label{p9:eq:log-potential}
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
 S_1(z)=L(2z+1)-L(2z-1).
 \label{p9:eq:S1-log-difference}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $z>1$, $\log(z-Z)$ is bounded, so
$S_\lambda(z)=\E\exp[-\lambda\log(z-Z)]$ is entire.  Expanding
$(1-Z/z)^{-\lambda}$ and using symmetry removes odd moments, proving
\cref{p9:eq:S-moment-series}.  Differentiation under the integral gives
\cref{p9:eq:S-derivative-hierarchy}.

The random series satisfies
\begin{equation}
 Z\overset d=\frac{V+Z'}2,
 \qquad V\sim\operatorname{Unif}[-1,1],
 \label{p9:eq:Z-smoothing}
\end{equation}
with $Z'$ an independent copy.  Conditioning on $V$ gives
\cref{p9:eq:S-integral-refinement}.  Changing variables turns its integral into
$\int_{2z-1}^{2z+1}S_\lambda(w)\dd w$.  Since
$S_{\lambda-1}'=(1-\lambda)S_\lambda$, this proves
\cref{p9:eq:S-order-lowering}.  Taking the removable limit at $\lambda=1$ and
using $L'=S_1$ gives \cref{p9:eq:S1-log-difference}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Interpolation between Appell polynomials and resolvents]
For $m\in\N_0$,
\begin{equation}
 S_{-m}(z)=\E(z-Z)^m
 \label{p9:eq:S-negative-polynomial}
\end{equation}
is a rational-coefficient Appell polynomial.  Positive integer orders are
ordinary higher resolvents.  Thus \cref{p9:eq:S-order-lowering} is a single
complex-order hierarchy connecting the polynomial and resolvent sides.
\end{remark}

\subsection{One-sided version}

Define, for $z>1$,
\begin{equation}
 R_\lambda(z)=\E(z-Y)^{-\lambda}.
 \label{p9:eq:R-lambda}
\end{equation}
Since $Y\overset d=(U+Y')/2$ with $U$ uniform on $[0,1]$,
\begin{align}
 R_\lambda(z)
 &=2^\lambda\int_0^1R_\lambda(2z-u)\dd u,
 \label{p9:eq:R-integral-refinement}\\
 &=\frac{2^\lambda}{1-\lambda}
   \left(R_{\lambda-1}(2z)-R_{\lambda-1}(2z-1)\right),
 \quad \lambda\ne1.
 \label{p9:eq:R-order-lowering}
\end{align}
The centered and one-sided transforms are related by
\begin{equation}
 S_\lambda(z)=2^{-\lambda}
 R_\lambda\!\left(\frac{z+1}{2}\right).
 \label{p9:eq:S-R-relation}
\end{equation}
For $\lambda>0$, $S_\lambda$ and $R_\lambda$ are completely monotone on their
real domains; all alternating derivatives are explicitly positive by
\cref{p9:eq:S-derivative-hierarchy}.

\section{Complex-order resolvents involving \texorpdfstring{$F$}{F} and the inverse}
\label{p9:sec:complex-resolvents}

\subsection{Distribution and survival resolvents}

For $z>1$ and $\lambda\ne1$, Fubini or integration by parts gives
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1\frac{F(x)}{(z-x)^\lambda}\dd x
 &=\frac{R_{\lambda-1}(z)-(z-1)^{1-\lambda}}{1-\lambda},
 \label{p9:eq:F-complex-resolvent}\\
 \int_0^1\frac{1-F(x)}{(z-x)^\lambda}\dd x
 &=\frac{z^{1-\lambda}-R_{\lambda-1}(z)}{1-\lambda}.
 \label{p9:eq:survival-complex-resolvent}
\end{align}
At $\lambda=1$, the removable limits are
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1\frac{F(x)}{z-x}\dd x
 &=\E\log(z-Y)-\log(z-1),
 \label{p9:eq:F-log-resolvent}\\
 \int_0^1\frac{1-F(x)}{z-x}\dd x
 &=\log z-\E\log(z-Y).
 \label{p9:eq:survival-log-resolvent}
\end{align}
These extend the integer resolvent formulas in the corpus to arbitrary complex
order.

\subsection{Inverse-composition resolvents}

\begin{theorem}[complex inverse-resolvent transmutation]
\label{p9:thm:inverse-resolvent}
Let $H$ be absolutely continuous on $[0,1]$, $z>1$, and $\lambda\ne1$.  Then
\begin{align}
 T_{\lambda,H}(z)
 &:=\int_0^1(z-y)^{-\lambda}H(Q(y))\dd y
 \label{p9:eq:T-def}\\
 &=\frac{H(0)z^{1-\lambda}-H(1)(z-1)^{1-\lambda}}
        {1-\lambda}
   +\frac1{1-\lambda}
    \int_0^1(z-F(x))^{1-\lambda}H'(x)\dd x.
 \label{p9:eq:T-transmutation}
\end{align}
At $\lambda=1$,
\begin{equation}
 T_{1,H}(z)
 =H(0)\log z-H(1)\log(z-1)
  +\int_0^1H'(x)\log(z-F(x))\dd x.
 \label{p9:eq:T-log-limit}
\end{equation}
Furthermore,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd^n}{\dd z^n}T_{\lambda,H}(z)
 =(-1)^n(\lambda)_nT_{\lambda+n,H}(z).
 \label{p9:eq:T-derivative-hierarchy}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Substitute $y=F(x)$:
\[
 T_{\lambda,H}(z)
 =\int_0^1(z-F(x))^{-\lambda}H(x)F'(x)\dd x.
\]
Since
$\dd(z-F(x))^{1-\lambda}=-(1-\lambda)
(z-F(x))^{-\lambda}F'(x)\dd x$, integration by parts proves
\cref{p9:eq:T-transmutation}.  Expanding at $1-\lambda=0$ gives
\cref{p9:eq:T-log-limit}; differentiation under the integral gives the hierarchy.
\end{proof}

For $H(x)=x^p$ with $p>0$,
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1(z-y)^{-\lambda}Q(y)^p\dd y
 =\frac{-(z-1)^{1-\lambda}
  +p\int_0^1x^{p-1}(z-F(x))^{1-\lambda}\dd x}{1-\lambda}.
 \label{p9:eq:Q-power-complex-resolvent}
\end{equation}
This is the complex-order extension of the inverse logarithmic resolvent found
in the latest corpus report.

\subsection{Large-\texorpdfstring{$z$}{z} mixed-moment series}

For $|z|>1$,
\begin{equation}
 T_{\lambda,H}(z)
 =z^{-\lambda}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}
  \frac{(\lambda)_n}{n!z^n}
  \int_0^1y^nH(Q(y))\dd y.
 \label{p9:eq:T-moment-series}
\end{equation}
For $H(x)=x^p$, integration by parts after $y=F(x)$ gives
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1y^nQ(y)^p\dd y
 =\frac1{n+1}
  -\frac{p}{n+1}\int_0^1x^{p-1}F(x)^{n+1}\dd x.
 \label{p9:eq:mixed-inverse-moment}
\end{equation}
Thus every coefficient is reduced to a beta-type Fabius integral.  When $p$
and $n$ are nonnegative integers, finite dyadic formulas and rational moments
can be inserted wherever the existing corpus machinery applies.

\section{Riesz potentials and the fractional Laplacian of \texorpdfstring{$\Up$}{up}}
\label{p9:sec:Riesz}

This section gives the cleanest new transform consequence.  Compact support
turns the nonlocal exterior fractional Laplacian into the generalized
Stieltjes hierarchy exactly, not merely asymptotically.

\subsection{Exact exterior fractional Laplacian}

For $0<\beta<2$, use the standard one-dimensional normalization
\cite{p9:Kwasnicki2017}:
\begin{equation}
 C_{1,\beta}
 =\frac{\beta2^{\beta-1}\Gamma((1+\beta)/2)}
       {\sqrt\pi\,\Gamma(1-\beta/2)}.
 \label{p9:eq:C1beta}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
 (-\Delta)^{\beta/2}f(x)
 =C_{1,\beta}\PV\int_{\R}
   \frac{f(x)-f(t)}{|x-t|^{1+\beta}}\dd t.
 \label{p9:eq:frac-lap-singular}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[exact exterior Stieltjes representation]
\label{p9:thm:exterior-Riesz}
For $0<\beta<2$ and $|x|>1$,
\begin{equation}
 (-\Delta)^{\beta/2}\Up(x)
 =-C_{1,\beta}S_{1+\beta}(|x|).
 \label{p9:eq:exterior-Riesz-Stieltjes}
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
 (-\Delta)^{\beta/2}\Up(x)
 =-C_{1,\beta}|x|^{-1-\beta}
  \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}
   \frac{(1+\beta)_{2k}}{(2k)!}
   \frac{c_k}{|x|^{2k}}.
 \label{p9:eq:exterior-Riesz-series}
\end{equation}
The first terms are
\begin{align}
 (-\Delta)^{\beta/2}\Up(x)
 ={}&-C_{1,\beta}|x|^{-1-\beta}
 \left[1+\frac{(1+\beta)(2+\beta)}{18x^2}\right.\nonumber\\
 &\left.\hspace{25mm}
 +\frac{19(1+\beta)(2+\beta)(3+\beta)(4+\beta)}{16200x^4}
 +O(x^{-6})\right].
 \label{p9:eq:exterior-Riesz-first}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $|x|>1$, $\Up(x)=0$ and the singularity is absent.  Thus
\[
 (-\Delta)^{\beta/2}\Up(x)
 =-C_{1,\beta}\int_{-1}^{1}
  \frac{\Up(t)}{|x-t|^{1+\beta}}\dd t.
\]
For $x>1$ this is $-C_{1,\beta}S_{1+\beta}(x)$.  Evenness gives the same formula
with $|x|$ for $x<-1$.  Insert \cref{p9:eq:S-moment-series} and the exact moments
\cref{p9:eq:first-c}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[sign and conservation]
The exterior fractional Laplacian is strictly negative.  At the center,
\begin{align}
 (-\Delta)^{\beta/2}\Up(0)
 &=C_{1,\beta}\int_{\R}\frac{1-\Up(t)}{|t|^{1+\beta}}\dd t\nonumber\\
 &=2C_{1,\beta}\left(
   \int_0^1\frac{1-\Up(t)}{t^{1+\beta}}\dd t+\frac1\beta
   \right)>0.
 \label{p9:eq:Riesz-center-positive}
\end{align}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
 \int_{\R}(-\Delta)^{\beta/2}\Up(x)\dd x=0.
 \label{p9:eq:Riesz-zero-mass}
\end{equation}
Hence at least one zero occurs on each half-axis.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The exterior sign follows from \cref{p9:eq:exterior-Riesz-Stieltjes}.  Since
$\Up(0)=1$, the singular-integral definition at $0$ gives the first line of
\cref{p9:eq:Riesz-center-positive}; splitting at $|t|=1$ gives the second.  The
function $(-\Delta)^{\beta/2}\Up$ is integrable because it is smooth on compact
sets and has the exterior decay \cref{p9:eq:exterior-Riesz-series}.  Its integral
is therefore its Fourier transform at $0$, namely
$|0|^\beta\widehat\Up(0)=0$.  Evenness and the opposite signs at $0$ and outside
the support force a zero on each half-axis.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Riesz potentials}

For $0<\alpha<1$, the Riesz potential with multiplier $|\xi|^{-\alpha}$ is
\begin{equation}
 (I_\alpha^{\mathrm R}f)(x)
 =\kappa_\alpha\int_{\R}|x-t|^{\alpha-1}f(t)\dd t,
 \qquad
 \kappa_\alpha=
 \frac{\Gamma((1-\alpha)/2)}
      {2^\alpha\sqrt\pi\,\Gamma(\alpha/2)}.
 \label{p9:eq:Riesz-potential}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[exterior Riesz-potential expansion]
For $0<\alpha<1$ and $|x|>1$,
\begin{equation}
 I_\alpha^{\mathrm R}\Up(x)
 =\kappa_\alpha S_{1-\alpha}(|x|)
 =\kappa_\alpha|x|^{\alpha-1}
  \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}
   \frac{(1-\alpha)_{2k}}{(2k)!}
   \frac{c_k}{|x|^{2k}}.
 \label{p9:eq:Riesz-potential-series}
\end{equation}
In particular, the leading far-field coefficient is exactly the unit mass of
$\Up$; all corrections are rational polynomials in $\alpha$.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{A numerical one-node phenomenon}

The accompanying experiment reconstructs $\Up$ from the infinite sinc product, then
evaluates the nonperiodic singular integral \cref{p9:eq:frac-lap-singular}.  A
symmetric second-difference removes the principal-value singularity and a
Gauss--Jacobi rule integrates the remaining $u^{1-\beta}$ weight.  On a
$601$-point profile grid, exactly one positive sign change in $(0,1)$ is
resolved for each tested order; Brent refinement gives the following
approximations (the displayed seven decimal places are stable under a moderate
change of quadrature order):
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}c@{\qquad}c@{}}
\toprule
$\beta$ & positive zero $a_\beta$\\
\midrule
$0.25$ & $0.6721595$\\
$0.50$ & $0.6100983$\\
$0.75$ & $0.5741282$\\
$1.00$ & $0.5506118$\\
$1.25$ & $0.5344913$\\
$1.50$ & $0.5232645$\\
$1.75$ & $0.5154602$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{conjecture}[one-node law]
\label{p9:conj:one-node}
For every $0<\beta<2$, the even function
$(-\Delta)^{\beta/2}\Up$ has exactly two simple zeros $\pm a_\beta$.
It is positive on $(-a_\beta,a_\beta)$ and negative outside.  Moreover,
$a_\beta$ is strictly decreasing, with
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{\beta\downarrow0}a_\beta=1,
 \qquad
 \lim_{\beta\uparrow2}a_\beta=\frac12.
 \label{p9:eq:one-node-limits}
\end{equation}
\end{conjecture}

The endpoint $1/2$ is natural: on $(0,1/2)$,
$-\Up''=F''=4F'(2x)>0$, while the integer second derivative changes its
Thue--Morse cell sign across $x=1/2$.  Proving uniqueness for noninteger order
will likely require a variation-diminishing or total-positivity principle for
the singular kernel combined with the special unimodality of $\Up$.

\section{Fractional energies and autocorrelation transforms}
\label{p9:sec:fractional-energy}

The integer derivative norm law in the corpus samples a continuous spectral
Mellin transform at even integers.  Define
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal E(s)
 =\frac1{2\pi}\int_{\R}|\xi|^s|\phi(\xi)|^2\dd\xi,
 \qquad \Re s>-1.
 \label{p9:eq:E-s}
\end{equation}
The sinc product decays faster than every power, so this is holomorphic in the
half-plane $\Re s>-1$.

\begin{proposition}[log-convex fractional energy]
For real $s>-1$, $\mathcal E(s)>0$ and $\log\mathcal E(s)$ is convex.  At even
integers,
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal E(2n)=\norm{\Up^{(n)}}_2^2
 =2^{n(n+1)}\mathcal E(0).
 \label{p9:eq:E-even}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for $2n\le s\le2n+2$,
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal E(s)
 \le \mathcal E(0)\,2^{(n+1)(s-n)}.
 \label{p9:eq:E-logconvex-bound}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Positivity is immediate.  Hölder's inequality applied to
$|\xi|^s|\phi|^2\dd\xi$ proves log-convexity.  Parseval gives the first equality
in \cref{p9:eq:E-even}; the second is the existing derivative-equimeasurability
law.  Convex interpolation between the values at $2n$ and $2n+2$ gives
\cref{p9:eq:E-logconvex-bound}.
\end{proof}

For a shift $a\in\R$, define the fractional autocorrelation
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal C_s(a)
 =\frac1{2\pi}\int_{\R}
  \e^{\ii\xi a}|\xi|^s|\phi(\xi)|^2\dd\xi.
 \label{p9:eq:C-s-a}
\end{equation}
Since $\Up$ is even,
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal C_s(a)
 =(-\Delta_a)^{s/2}(\Up*\Up)(a),
 \qquad
 \mathcal C_s(0)=\mathcal E(s).
 \label{p9:eq:C-fractional-correlation}
\end{equation}
Thus the entire family of fractional Sobolev energies and shifted correlations
is encoded by the same lacunary sinc product.  For $s=2n$, this reduces to
integer derivatives; for noninteger $s$, it is a new continuous interpolation
problem closely related to the shifted Stieltjes--Wigert spectral measure in
the derivative-norm report.

\section{Definite integrals and transform catalog}
\label{p9:sec:definite-catalog}

This section gathers a practical set of exact reductions.  Most of the first
group is already in the corpus; several composition identities at the end are
new elementary consequences.

\subsection{CDF--survival master identities}

Let $G$ be absolutely continuous on $[0,1]$.  Then
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1G'(x)F(x)\dd x
 &=G(1)-\E G(Y),
 \label{p9:eq:CDF-master}\\
 \int_0^1G'(x)(1-F(x))\dd x
 &=\E G(Y)-G(0).
 \label{p9:eq:survival-master}
\end{align}
Examples include
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1x^{s-1}F(x)\dd x
 &=\frac{1-\Mell(s)}s,
 \label{p9:eq:Mellin-F}\\
 \int_0^1x^{s-1}\Up(x)\dd x
 &=\frac{\Mell(s)}s,
 \label{p9:eq:Mellin-half-up}\\
 \int_0^1\e^{tx}F(x)\dd x
 &=\frac{\e^t-P(t)}t,
 \label{p9:eq:Laplace-F}\\
 \int_0^1\e^{tx}\Up(x)\dd x
 &=\frac{P(t)-1}t.
 \label{p9:eq:Laplace-half-up}
\end{align}
The apparent singularities at $s=0$ or $t=0$ are removable.  Differentiating
in the parameters generates logarithmic and polynomially weighted families.

\subsection{Quantile transport and power duality}

For every integrable $H$,
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1H(Q(y))\dd y
 =\int_0^1H(x)F'(x)\dd x
 =\E H(Y).
 \label{p9:eq:quantile-transport}
\end{equation}
For $\Re p,\Re q>0$,
\begin{equation}
 p\int_0^1x^{p-1}F(x)^q\dd x
 +q\int_0^1y^{q-1}Q(y)^p\dd y=1.
 \label{p9:eq:power-duality}
\end{equation}
The universal beta family is
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1F(x)^a(1-F(x))^bF'(x)\dd x
 =B(a+1,b+1),
 \qquad \Re a,\Re b>-1.
 \label{p9:eq:universal-beta}
\end{equation}
Using $F'=2\Up(2x-1)$,
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1F(x)^a\Up(x)^b\Up(2x-1)\dd x
 =\frac12B(a+1,b+1).
 \label{p9:eq:up-beta-combination}
\end{equation}
This gives, for example,
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1F(x)(1-F(x))F'(x)\dd x=\frac16.
 \label{p9:eq:F-survival-density}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Full-line analytic kernels and Fourier transforms}

If $h(t)=\sum_{n\ge0}a_nt^n$ is entire, then
\begin{equation}
 \int_{-1}^1h(t)\Up(t)\dd t
 =\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{2k}c_k.
 \label{p9:eq:entire-kernel-up}
\end{equation}
The Fourier, bilateral Laplace, Bessel, and generalized Stieltjes transforms
are therefore
\begin{align}
 \int_{-1}^1\e^{\ii\xi t}\Up(t)\dd t
 &=\prod_{j\ge1}\sincPartcc(\xi/2^j),
 \label{p9:eq:Fourier-up-catalog}\\
 \int_{-1}^1\e^{st}\Up(t)\dd t
 &=\prod_{j\ge1}\frac{\sinh(s/2^j)}{s/2^j},
 \label{p9:eq:bilateral-Laplace-up}\\
 \int_{-1}^1J_0(at)\Up(t)\dd t
 &=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}
   \frac{(-1)^k(a^2/4)^k}{(k!)^2}c_k,
 \label{p9:eq:Bessel-up}\\
 \int_{-1}^1(z-t)^{-\lambda}\Up(t)\dd t
 &=S_\lambda(z).
 \label{p9:eq:Stieltjes-catalog}
\end{align}

\subsection{Product and order-statistic integrals}

Let $m,n\ge1$, let $Y_1,Y_2,\ldots$ be independent copies of $Y$,
$A_m=\max(Y_1,\ldots,Y_m)$, and
$B_n=\min(Y_{m+1},\ldots,Y_{m+n})$.  Then
\begin{equation}
 F(x)^m(1-F(x))^n
 =\Prob(A_m\le x<B_n),
 \label{p9:eq:order-gap-pointwise}
\end{equation}
and hence, for real $p>-1$,
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1x^pF(x)^m(1-F(x))^n\dd x
 =\frac1{p+1}\E\left[(B_n^{p+1}-A_m^{p+1})_+\right].
 \label{p9:eq:order-gap-integral}
\end{equation}
This is an exact probabilistic closed form even when no reduction to rational
moments is available.  The Lambert endpoint theory controls its large-$m$ or
large-$n$ regimes.

\subsection{Compositions with the inverse density}

Equation~\ref{p9:eq:Qprime} yields a useful family that appears not to have been
isolated in the audited integral reports.  For $p>-1$,
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1\left(F'(Q(y))\right)^p\dd y
 &=\int_0^1F'(x)^{p+1}\dd x
 =2^p\int_{-1}^1\Up(t)^{p+1}\dd t,
 \label{p9:eq:inverse-density-composition}\\
 \int_0^1\Up(2Q(y)-1)^p\dd y
 &=\int_{-1}^1\Up(t)^{p+1}\dd t,
 \label{p9:eq:up-quantile-composition}\\
 \int_0^1Q'(y)^{-p}\dd y
 &=2^p\norm{\Up}_{p+1}^{p+1}.
 \label{p9:eq:Qprime-negative-power}
\end{align}
The proof is the single substitution $y=F(x)$ followed by $t=2x-1$.
These formulas tie inverse-derivative integrals directly to the complete
$L^p$ spectrum of the compact density.

\section{Indefinite integral catalog}
\label{p9:sec:indefinite-catalog}

To avoid ambiguity, an ``indefinite integral'' below means the normalized
primitive with zero initial data; arbitrary constants or lower-degree
polynomials can be restored afterward.

\subsection{Fabius and reflected Fabius functions}

For $0\le x\le1$,
\begin{align}
 \int_0^xF(t)\dd t
 &=F(x/2),
 \label{p9:eq:first-F-antiderivative}\\
 J^nF(x)
 &=2^{\binom n2}F(x/2^n),
 \label{p9:eq:nth-F-antiderivative}\\
 \int_0^x(1-F(t))\dd t
 &=x-F(x/2),
 \label{p9:eq:first-survival-antiderivative}\\
 J^n(1-F)(x)
 &=\frac{x^n}{n!}-2^{\binom n2}F(x/2^n).
 \label{p9:eq:nth-survival-antiderivative}
\end{align}
Because $F(1-x)=1-F(x)$, these are also primitives of the reflected Fabius
function.  Polynomial and arbitrary complex-power weights are supplied by
\cref{p9:eq:weighted-newton-volterra}.

\subsection{Rvachev folds}

On $[-1,0]$, $\Up(x)=F(x+1)$, while on $[0,1]$,
$\Up(x)=1-F(x)$.  Thus a normalized first primitive based at $-1$ is
\begin{equation}
 \int_{-1}^x\Up(t)\dd t
 =\begin{cases}
 F((x+1)/2),&-1\le x\le0,\\[1mm]
 1-F((1-x)/2),&0\le x\le1,\\[1mm]
 1,&x\ge1.
 \end{cases}
 \label{p9:eq:up-first-piecewise}
\end{equation}
Higher integer primitives follow either by repeated use of
\cref{p9:eq:nth-F-antiderivative} on each fold or from the exact truncated moment
\begin{equation}
 I_{-1+}^n\Up(x)
 =\frac1{(n-1)!}\E[(x-Z)_+^{n-1}].
 \label{p9:eq:up-n-incomplete-moment}
\end{equation}
For $x\ge1$ this becomes an Appell polynomial in $x$ with rational
coefficients.

\subsection{Inverse Fabius function}

The normalized first primitive is \cref{p9:eq:Q-first-primitive}.  More generally,
for $\alpha>0$ and $p>0$,
\begin{equation}
 I_{0+}^\alpha Q^p(y)
 =\frac{p}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}
   \int_0^{Q(y)}x^{p-1}(y-F(x))^\alpha\dd x,
 \label{p9:eq:inverse-fractional-antiderivative-catalog}
\end{equation}
while the Fabius-clock primitive is the elementary gamma law
\cref{p9:eq:Q-clock-power-I}.  These two answers are complementary: the first
retains Euclidean probability time and has a Fabius kernel; the second uses
the inverse clock and becomes algebraic.

\section{Numerical experiments and reproducibility}
\label{p9:sec:numerics}

The accompanying script \texttt{numerical\_experiments.py} reconstructs
$\Up$ by FFT inversion of a $48$-factor sinc product on a $2^{18}$ grid,
builds monotone interpolants for $F$ and $Q$, and compares formulas obtained
from mathematically independent representations.  The fractional-Laplacian
profiles are then evaluated by the nonperiodic singular integral, not by a
periodic Fourier multiplier, eliminating algebraic image contamination.
Exact centered moments are generated by rational recurrence rather than by
numerical quadrature.  No random sampling is used.

\section{Conjectures and frontier directions}
\label{p9:sec:frontiers}

\subsection{Phase-resolved Caputo endpoint correction}

The proof of \cref{p9:thm:Q-fractional-endpoint} discards the narrow layer
$t\approx1$ where $F(qt)/F(q)$ is non-negligible.  The width of that layer is
controlled by the same saddle parameter that appears in the inverse
Lambert--$W$ expansion.

\begin{conjecture}[Caputo endpoint phase]
\label{p9:conj:Caputo-phase}
For fixed $r>0$ and $0<\beta<1$, there exists a bounded one-periodic function
$A_{r,\beta}$ of the repository's endpoint phase $\vartheta(y)$ such that
\begin{equation}
 \frac{{}^{\Ccap}D_{0+}^\beta Q(y)^r}
 {Q(y)^r y^{-\beta}/\Gamma(1-\beta)}
 =1+\frac{A_{r,\beta}(\vartheta(y))}{\rho(y)}
  +O\!\left(\rho(y)^{-2}\right),
 \label{p9:eq:Caputo-phase-conjecture}
\end{equation}
where $\rho(y)$ is defined by \cref{p9:eq:rho-y}.  The periodic term should be
nonconstant but extremely small, as in the endpoint density asymptotics.
\end{conjecture}

A route to proof is a boundary-layer rescaling of
\cref{p9:eq:Caputo-endpoint-ratio}, using the all-orders ratio asymptotic for
$F(qt)/F(q)$ uniformly when $1-t=O(\rho(y)^{-1})$.

\subsection{The one-node law and total positivity}

Conjecture \ref{p9:conj:one-node} is stronger than the sign information proved in
\cref{p9:sec:Riesz}.  Three plausible approaches are:
\begin{enumerate}
\item represent the interior fractional Laplacian as a signed Stieltjes
      convolution and prove a variation-diminishing property;
\item use the infinite convolution construction of $\Up$ and preservation of
      single crossing under uniform averaging;
\item establish strict total positivity for the appropriate fractional kernel
      after subtracting the center value.
\end{enumerate}
A certified interval-arithmetic computation over a dense mesh in $\beta$ would
be a useful intermediate theorem, but not a substitute for uniqueness.

\subsection{Fractional spectral interpolation}

At the even integers,
\begin{equation}
 \log_2\frac{\mathcal E(2n)}{\mathcal E(0)}=n(n+1).
 \label{p9:eq:E-quadratic-samples}
\end{equation}
The explicit spectral measure from the derivative-norm report is a
nonclassical representative of a shifted Stieltjes--Wigert moment problem.
Determining the full Mellin transform $\mathcal E(s)$ would interpolate those
quadratic samples and could reveal a small $2$-periodic correction to the
quadratic envelope
\begin{equation}
 \frac{s^2}{4}+\frac{s}{2}.
 \label{p9:eq:E-quadratic-envelope}
\end{equation}
The exact integer samples alone do not force such periodicity; this is an
open spectral-analysis direction rather than a formal conjecture at present.

\subsection{General base and \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-deformation}

Replace $2^{-j}$ by normalized $b^{-j}$ weights.  The clock conjugacy,
inverse transmutation, generalized Stieltjes hierarchy, and exterior Riesz
expansion survive with minimal change.  The order-lowering factors become
$b$-dependent, while the rational moment coefficients become $q=1/b$
objects.  The resulting family should provide a direct fractional-calculus
bridge between the base-$b$ random series and Gaussian $q$-binomial
arithmetic.

\subsection{Arithmetic of fractional constants}

For rational $\beta$ and algebraic $z>1$, the values
\begin{equation}
 S_\beta(z),
 \qquad
 {}^{\Ccap}D_{0+}^\beta Q(y)^r,
 \qquad
 \mathcal E(\beta),
 \qquad
 a_\beta
 \label{p9:eq:fractional-constants}
\end{equation}
are natural new constants.  The Stieltjes series expresses the first through
rational moments and gamma-polynomial coefficients.  Pad\'e approximation,
Mahler-type functional equations, and denominator estimates from the
$q$-binomial formulas may offer arithmetic information, although no
irrationality claim is currently justified.

\section{Lean formalization roadmap}
\label{p9:sec:Lean}

The latest repository merge already formalizes the normalized integer
Volterra operator and the exact Fabius primitive ladder.  The new results can
be layered on top in increasing order of analytic difficulty.

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Composition conjugacy.}  Define $\mathcal C_F$ and
      $\mathcal C_Q$ on suitable function spaces; prove they are inverse
      order isomorphisms and transport semigroups.
\item \textbf{Fabius-clock integrals.}  Formalize
      \cref{p9:eq:IF-kernel} first for positive real orders and continuous
      functions using the substitution theorem.  The power law then reduces
      to Mathlib's beta integral.
\item \textbf{First-order Caputo transmutation.}  The proof of
      \cref{p9:eq:inverse-Caputo-transmutation} requires only absolute
      continuity, inverse differentiation on $(0,1)$, and a monotone change of
      variables.  This is likely the most accessible genuinely new theorem.
\item \textbf{Endpoint asymptotics.}  Package rapid variation and convexity
      into a dominated-convergence lemma for kernels
      $t^{r-1}(1-F(qt)/F(q))^{-\beta}$.
\item \textbf{Generalized Stieltjes hierarchy.}  Formalize the bounded random
      variable $Z$, complex powers for real $z>1$, the moment series, and the
      smoothing equation.  The order-lowering identity is then elementary.
\item \textbf{Exterior Riesz theorem.}  Avoid principal values entirely by
      restricting first to $|x|>1$; compact support reduces the proof to an
      ordinary integral and the Stieltjes identity.
\item \textbf{Weyl and full Riesz operators.}  These require Fourier
      multiplier and tempered-distribution infrastructure and are a later
      target.
\end{enumerate}

The numerical script can be converted into certified rational interval checks
for selected transform identities and the conjectural zero locations, but the
formal statements should remain independent of floating-point evidence.

\section{Conclusion}

The ordinary integral theory of the Fabius--Rvachev system is already unusually
rigid: differentiation is conjugate to dyadic dilation, integer integration
runs backward along the same orbit, and inverse substitution converts many
areas into moments.  The present investigation shows that this rigidity
continues in several genuinely fractional directions.

The Fabius and inverse clocks give exact conjugate fractional calculi with
semigroups, gamma power laws, and Mittag--Leffler eigenfunctions.  In ordinary
time, the inverse-composition transmutation removes the singular derivative of
$Q$ and replaces it by a Fabius-kernel integral; this leads to rigorous
fractional endpoint laws.  Standard fractional derivatives of $F$ are exact
fractional primitives of $\Up$, while whole-line Weyl derivatives satisfy a
complex-order refinement hierarchy.  On the transform side, one entire
function $S_\lambda(z)$ unifies Appell polynomials, Cauchy resolvents, logarithmic
potentials, Riesz tails, and fractional-Laplacian exterior fields.

The strongest next targets are now sharply posed: prove the one-node law,
resolve the first phase-dependent correction to the inverse Caputo endpoint
asymptotic, determine the continuous fractional-energy interpolation of the
integer derivative norms, and formalize the first-order transmutation and
Stieltjes hierarchy in Lean.  None requires inventing a new ad hoc numerical
object; each is anchored in an exact identity proved above.

\section{Exact low-order moments}

For convenience, the first one-sided moments are
\begin{equation}
 m_0=1,
 \quad m_1=\frac12,
 \quad m_2=\frac5{18},
 \quad m_3=\frac16,
 \quad m_4=\frac{143}{1350},
 \quad m_5=\frac{19}{270}.
 \label{p9:eq:low-moments-appendix}
\end{equation}
The centered moments in \cref{p9:eq:first-c} satisfy
\begin{equation}
 c_k=\frac{2m_{2k+1}}{2k+1}.
 \label{p9:eq:c-m-relation}
\end{equation}
Substitution in \cref{p9:eq:S-moment-series} produces exact rational-coefficient
large-$z$ expansions for every fixed complex order $\lambda$.

\part{Integral}
% Absorbed from Fabius_Fractional_Integral_Transform_Frontiers/fabius_fractional_integral_transform_frontiers.tex




\begin{abstract}
This report develops a broad integral calculus for the Fabius function \(\F\), its
inverse \(\Q=\F^{-1}\), and Rvachev's compactly supported function \(\upf\), after a
recursive audit of the current and archived LaTeX corpus under the ProveIt documentation
root.  Existing dyadic derivative formulas, exact moment arithmetic, q-binomial and
Thue--Morse structure, sinc products, Lambert--\(W\) endpoint expansions, Bell and
Bernoulli polynomial systems, repeated integration, weighted primitives, inverse-area
identities, and the known obstruction to naive fractional self-similarity are treated as
baseline results rather than reannounced as new.

The report first synthesizes ordinary and weighted antiderivatives, definite integrals,
entire Mellin moments, negative and complex powers, Laplace/Fourier/Mellin/Stieltjes
transforms, inverse-function dualities, order-statistic identities, and all integer
orders of differentiation and integration.  It then advances beyond the audited corpus
in four directions.  First, the inverse Fabius endpoint is placed in a fractional
calculus: for \(T=\log(1/y)\) and
\(\eta=y\Q'(y)/\Q(y)\sim\sqrt{\log2/(2T)}\),
\[
 I_{0+}^{a}(\Q^\beta)(y)
 =\frac{y^a\Q(y)^\beta}{\Gamma(a+1)}
  \bigl(1-\beta H_a\eta+o(\eta)\bigr),
\]
while the Riemann--Liouville/Caputo derivative satisfies the dual digamma correction and
the sharp strong threshold
\(D_{0+}^{\sigma}(\Q^\beta)\in L^p\iff \sigma p\le1\) near the endpoint.  Second,
exact exterior moment series are obtained for the Riesz potential and fractional
Laplacian of \(\upf\); all coefficients are generated rationally from Bernoulli
cumulants through complete Bell polynomials.  Third, the Cauchy transform is shown to
obey a logarithmic smoothing fixed point and an all-order resolvent hierarchy.  Fourth,
the log-periodic obstruction to fractional dyadic scaling is converted into an exact
Floquet multiplier cocycle that restores the continuous dilation orbit on the global
signed Fabius extension.

A deterministic, fully commented Python program reconstructs \(\upf\) from its infinite
sinc product and checks the new nonlocal series, transform identities, and fractional
endpoint corrections.  ``New'' means not found in the recursively audited repository
corpus at the time of writing; no claim of worldwide priority is made without a separate
literature proof.
\end{abstract}


\bigskip

\section{Corpus audit, scope, and novelty boundary}

\subsection{What was reviewed}

The repository was traversed recursively through the documentation root, including the
main monographs, glossary, Lean walkthrough, non-elementarity article, papers, canonical
research-frontier volume, current frontier drafts, auxiliary generated TeX fragments, and
frozen archive.  The archive map was used to identify historical duplicates and their
canonical successors; living sources and current drafts were checked directly at theorem
and section level, with integration-specific full-text searches described in
Appendix~\ref{p10:app:complete-manifest}.

The audit is anchored to the path-complete inventory at commit
\path{32d6d36c51d803289e6d6a0dc0c37753766eba47}, which records exactly sixty-seven
LaTeX paths.  A second traversal of the live main branch found five later frontier packages
adding eleven visible TeX paths, including the self-sampling report's six generated table
fragments.  Thus the current ledger contains seventy-eight paths: five core
synthesis/reference sources, thirty-two frozen historical sources, thirty-four active
frontier-report or auxiliary sources, and seven paper transcriptions
\cite{p10:ProveIt,p10:ProveItFrontiers,p10:ProveItInventory}.

The following table states the boundary used in this report.

\toprule
\textbf{Imported baseline} & \textbf{How it is used here}\\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule

\begin{resultbox}[Strongest corpus-relative frontier results]
After comparison with all seventy-eight paths, the following proved package was not located
explicitly in the audited corpus: the Riemann--Liouville inverse-pair duality of
\cref{p10:thm:fractional-duality}; the digamma/trigamma endpoint expansions and sharp critical
strong-\(L^p\) law for \(\Q^\beta\) in
\cref{p10:thm:Q-fractional-primitive-asymptotic,p10:thm:Q-fractional-derivative-Lp}; the exact
exterior fractional-Laplacian and Riesz-potential series and the fractional Sobolev-energy
identity in \cref{p10:thm:up-fraclap-exterior,p10:thm:up-Riesz-exterior,p10:thm:up-fractional-energy};
the logarithmic Cauchy-transform fixed point and all-order resolvent hierarchy of
\cref{p10:thm:Cauchy-fixed-hierarchy}; and the Floquet multiplier/cocycle that repairs
fractional dyadic scaling in \cref{p10:thm:Floquet-corrector}.  The sinc-product Gini identity
of \cref{p10:thm:gini-sinc} is another compact deduction not found in the comparison searches.
The universal inverse-pair identity, entire moment/Newton calculus, negative-moment
recurrence, beta transforms, and related ordinary formulas are presented as a useful
self-contained synthesis and auxiliary deductions, without a priority claim.
\end{resultbox}

\subsection{Conventions}

All powers of positive real variables use the real logarithm:
\(x^s=\exp(s\log x)\).  The totalized Fabius distribution function is
\[
 \F(x)=0\quad(x\le0),\qquad \F(x)=1\quad(x\ge1).
\]
Its inverse \(\Q:(0,1)\to(0,1)\) is the ordinary increasing inverse; endpoint values
are understood by continuity.  Angular frequency is used for Fourier transforms:
\(\widehat f(t)=\int_\R e^{itx}f(x)\dd x\).

\section{Probability model and transform baseline}

\subsection{The dyadic uniform sum}

Let \(U_1,U_2,\ldots\) be independent random variables uniformly distributed on
\([0,1]\), and put
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Ydef}
 Y=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{U_k}{2^k},
 \qquad
 Z=2Y-1=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{2U_k-1}{2^k}.
\end{equation}
Then \(Y\in[0,1]\), \(Z\in[-1,1]\), and the laws are symmetric:
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:symmetry-laws}
 Y\overset d=1-Y,
 \qquad Z\overset d=-Z.
\end{equation}
The Fabius function is the distribution function of \(Y\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Fdist}
 \F(x)=\Prob(Y\le x),
\end{equation}
and \(\upf\) is the density of \(Z\).  Thus
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:F-up-relations}
 \F'(x)=2\upf(2x-1),\qquad
 \upf(x)=
 \begin{cases}
   \F(x+1),&-1\le x\le0,\\
   \F(1-x)=1-\F(x),&0\le x\le1,\\
   0,&|x|>1.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
In particular \(\F(x)+\F(1-x)=1\) on \([0,1]\), and the quantile obeys
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Qsymmetry}
 \Q(1-v)=1-\Q(v).
\end{equation}

The self-decomposition
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:smoothing}
 Y\overset d=\frac{U+Y'}2
\end{equation}
holds with \(U\sim\mathrm{Unif}[0,1]\) independent of a copy \(Y'\) of \(Y\).
It will generate both the positive- and negative-moment recurrences.

\subsection{Moment-generating and Fourier products}

Define
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:P-U-products}
 P(t)=\E e^{tY},\qquad \mathcal U(t)=\E e^{tZ}.
\end{equation}
Independence in \cref{p10:eq:Ydef} gives the locally uniformly convergent products
\begin{align}
 P(t)&=\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}
 \frac{e^{t/2^k}-1}{t/2^k},\label{p10:eq:P-product}\\
 \mathcal U(t)&=\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}
 \frac{\sinh(t/2^k)}{t/2^k},\label{p10:eq:U-product}\\
 P(t)&=e^{t/2}\mathcal U(t/2).\label{p10:eq:P-U-relation}
\end{align}
The characteristic function of the up law is therefore
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:phi-product}
 \phi(t)=\widehat{\upf}(t)
 =\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\sin(t/2^k)}{t/2^k}.
\end{equation}
The scaling equation
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:P-pantograph}
 P(2t)=\frac{e^t-1}{t}P(t)
\end{equation}
is the exponential generating-function form of the moment recurrence below.

\subsection{Rational moments, cumulants, and Bell polynomials}

Write
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:mn-mun}
 m_n=\E Y^n=\int_0^1\Q(v)^n\dd v,
 \qquad
 \mu_n=\E Z^n=\int_{-1}^1x^n\upf(x)\dd x.
\end{equation}
Taking the \(n\)-th moment in \cref{p10:eq:smoothing} gives
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:moment-recurrence}
 (2^n-1)m_n
 =\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\binom nk\frac{m_k}{n-k+1},
 \qquad m_0=1.
\end{equation}
Thus every \(m_n\) is rational.  The first values are
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:first-moments}
 1,\ \frac12,\ \frac5{18},\ \frac16,\ \frac{143}{1350},\
 \frac{19}{270},\ \frac{1153}{23814},\ \frac{583}{17010},\ldots.
\end{equation}

The logarithm of \cref{p10:eq:U-product} and
\(
 \log(\sinh z/z)
 =\sum_{r\ge1}2^{2r-1}B_{2r}z^{2r}/(r(2r)!)
\)
give the cumulants
\begin{align}
 \kappa_1(Y)&=\frac12,\nonumber\\
 \kappa_{2r}(Y)&=\frac{B_{2r}}{2r(2^{2r}-1)},
 &\kappa_{2r+1}(Y)&=0\quad(r\ge1),\label{p10:eq:Y-cumulants}\\
 \kappa_{2r}(Z)&=\frac{2^{2r}B_{2r}}{2r(2^{2r}-1)},
 &\kappa_{2r+1}(Z)&=0.\label{p10:eq:Z-cumulants}
\end{align}
Consequently \(m_n\) and \(\mu_n\) are complete exponential Bell polynomials in
these cumulants.  A useful bridge between the one-sided and centered moments is
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:mu-m-relation}
 \mu_{2j}
 =2\int_0^1x^{2j}\upf(x)\dd x
 =\frac{2m_{2j+1}}{2j+1},
 \qquad \mu_{2j+1}=0.
\end{equation}
For example
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:first-central-moments}
 \mu_2=\frac19,\qquad
 \mu_4=\frac{19}{675},\qquad
 \mu_6=\frac{583}{59535}.
\end{equation}

\section{A universal integration calculus for \texorpdfstring{\(\F\)}{F} and
\texorpdfstring{\(\Q\)}{Q}}

The classical area identity for a function and its inverse is only the first member of
a much larger family.  The following theorem is the main algebraic engine of the report.

\begin{theorem}[two-function inverse-pair identity]\label{p10:thm:AB}
Let \(A,B\) be absolutely continuous on \([0,1]\).  For every \(z\in[0,1]\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:AB-incomplete}
 A(z)B(\F(z))-A(0)B(0)
 =\int_0^z A'(x)B(\F(x))\dd x
 +\int_0^{\F(z)}A(\Q(v))B'(v)\dd v.
\end{equation}
At \(z=1\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:AB-complete}
 A(1)B(1)-A(0)B(0)
 =\int_0^1A'(x)B(\F(x))\dd x
 +\int_0^1A(\Q(v))B'(v)\dd v.
\end{equation}
The formulas remain valid under the usual improper-integral hypotheses whenever an
endpoint is singular.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate the product \(A(x)B(\F(x))\), integrate from \(0\) to \(z\), and in
the term containing \(\F'(x)\) use the increasing substitution \(v=\F(x)\),
so that \(x=\Q(v)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
No special functional equation of the Fabius function is needed in
\cref{p10:thm:AB}; it is a quantile identity.  What is special here is that the moments and
transforms of this particular quantile can be calculated by dyadic products and rational
recurrences.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Power duality and logarithmic derivatives}

Taking \(A(x)=x^p\), \(B(v)=v^q\) gives the following Mellin-type duality.

\begin{corollary}[power duality]\label{p10:cor:power-duality}
Initially for \(\Re p,\Re q>0\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:power-incomplete}
 p\int_0^z x^{p-1}\F(x)^q\dd x
 +q\int_0^{\F(z)}\Q(v)^p v^{q-1}\dd v
 =z^p\F(z)^q.
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:power-complete}
 p\int_0^1x^{p-1}\F(x)^q\dd x
 +q\int_0^1v^{q-1}\Q(v)^p\dd v=1.
\end{equation}
Both sides admit meromorphic or entire continuation in parameters whenever the endpoint
flatness cancels the apparent singularities.
\end{corollary}

For all logarithmic derivatives at once, define
\begin{align}
 J_{r,s}(p,q)&=\int_0^1x^{p-1}(\log x)^r\F(x)^q(\log\F(x))^s\dd x,\label{p10:eq:Jrs}\\
 K_{r,s}(p,q)&=\int_0^1v^{q-1}(\log v)^s\Q(v)^p(\log\Q(v))^r\dd v.\label{p10:eq:Krs}
\end{align}
Differentiating \cref{p10:eq:power-complete} gives, for \(r+s>0\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:log-hierarchy}
 pJ_{r,s}+rJ_{r-1,s}+qK_{r,s}+sK_{r,s-1}=0,
\end{equation}
with \(pJ_{0,0}+qK_{0,0}=1\).  Equation \cref{p10:eq:log-hierarchy} is a compact
general formula for every mixed derivative of the two Mellin kernels.

\subsection{A bivariate Laplace--Fourier identity}

The exponential choice \(A(x)=e^{sx}\), \(B(v)=e^{tv}\) produces an especially
simple transform relation.

\begin{theorem}[bivariate inverse-pair transform]\label{p10:thm:bivariate-exp}
For all \(s,t\in\C\) and \(z\in[0,1]\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:bivariate-incomplete}
 s\int_0^z e^{sx+t\F(x)}\dd x
 +t\int_0^{\F(z)}e^{tv+s\Q(v)}\dd v
 =e^{sz+t\F(z)}-1.
\end{equation}
Hence
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:bivariate-complete}
 s\int_0^1e^{sx+t\F(x)}\dd x
 +t\int_0^1e^{tv+s\Q(v)}\dd v
 =e^{s+t}-1.
\end{equation}
The identities are entire in \((s,t)\); the cases \(s=0\) or \(t=0\) are understood
by continuity.
\end{theorem}

Every mixed power integral involving \(x\) and \(\F(x)\), together with its quantile
dual, is obtained by coefficient extraction from \cref{p10:eq:bivariate-complete}.  Purely
imaginary \(s,t\) give a bivariate Fourier identity.

\subsection{Weighted antiderivatives}

Taking \(B(v)=v\) and a primitive \(A\) of a prescribed weight yields the useful
master formula
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:weighted-antiderivative-master}
 \int_0^x A'(t)\F(t)\dd t
 =A(x)\F(x)-A(0)\F(0)-\int_0^{\F(x)}A(\Q(v))\dd v.
\end{equation}
For monomial weights this becomes the promised indefinite integral.

\begin{corollary}[monomial--Fabius antiderivative]\label{p10:cor:monomial-F}
For \(p\ne-1\), initially where the integrals converge and then by continuation,
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:monomial-F-antiderivative}
 \int_0^x t^p\F(t)\dd t
 =\frac{x^{p+1}\F(x)-\displaystyle\int_0^{\F(x)}\Q(v)^{p+1}\dd v}{p+1}.
\end{equation}
The value at \(p=-1\) is removable:
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:F-over-x-incomplete}
 \int_0^x\frac{\F(t)}t\dd t
 =\F(x)\log x-\int_0^{\F(x)}\log\Q(v)\dd v.
\end{equation}
On \([0,1]\), where \(\upf=1-\F\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:monomial-up-half-antiderivative}
 \int_0^x t^p\upf(t)\dd t
 =\frac{x^{p+1}(1-\F(x))+\displaystyle\int_0^{\F(x)}\Q(v)^{p+1}\dd v}{p+1}.
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

More generally, from \cref{p10:eq:power-incomplete},
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Fpower-antiderivative}
 \int_0^x t^{p-1}\F(t)^q\dd t
 =\frac{x^p\F(x)^q}{p}
 -\frac{q}{p}\int_0^{\F(x)}\Q(v)^p v^{q-1}\dd v.
\end{equation}
Thus products of \(\F\) and the positive half of \(\upf\) reduce to beta-weighted
quantile integrals after expanding \((1-\F)^n\).

\section{The entire complex moment function}

\begin{definition}\label{p10:def:M}
For \(s\in\C\), set
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Mdef}
 \MPartiii(s)=\E Y^s=\int_0^1\Q(v)^s\dd v.
\end{equation}
For nonnegative integers, \(\MPartiii(n)=m_n\).
\end{definition}

\subsection{Entire continuation}

The endpoint flatness of \(\F\) is much stronger than what is needed for ordinary
positive moments: for every \(N>0\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:F-flat-bound}
 \F(x)=O_N(x^N)\qquad(x\downarrow0).
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[entire quantile Mellin function]\label{p10:thm:entireM}
The function \(\MPartiii\) in \cref{p10:eq:Mdef} is entire.  For every \(r\ge0\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:M-derivatives}
 \MPartiii^{(r)}(s)=\int_0^1\Q(v)^s(\log\Q(v))^r\dd v
 =\E\bigl[Y^s(\log Y)^r\bigr].
\end{equation}
In particular all negative moments and all logarithmic moments exist.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let \(K\subset\C\) be compact and choose \(A>0\) with \(\Re s\ge-A\) on \(K\).
For every fixed \(r\), the integrand is bounded in modulus by
\(Y^{-A}|\log Y|^r\).  Integration by parts gives
\[
 \E Y^{-A}=1+A\int_0^1x^{-A-1}\F(x)\dd x<\infty,
\]
using \cref{p10:eq:F-flat-bound} with an exponent larger than \(A\); inserting powers of
\(|\log x|\) does not alter convergence.  Dominated differentiation on compact sets
proves holomorphy and \cref{p10:eq:M-derivatives}.
\end{proof}

A first transform triangle follows immediately:
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1x^{s-1}\F(x)\dd x&=\frac{1-\MPartiii(s)}s,
 \label{p10:eq:Mellin-F}\\
 \int_0^1x^{s-1}\upf(x)\dd x&=\frac{\MPartiii(s)}s,
 \label{p10:eq:Mellin-up-half}\\
 \int_0^1\Q(v)^{s-1}\dd v&=\MPartiii(s-1).
 \label{p10:eq:Mellin-Q}
\end{align}
The first quotient has a removable singularity at \(s=0\) and is entire; the second
is the meromorphic continuation of the classical Mellin transform of \(\upf|_{[0,1]}\)
and has exactly one pole, at \(s=0\), with residue \(1\).  Evenness gives
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:absolute-Mellin-up}
 \int_{-1}^1|x|^{s-1}\upf(x)\dd x=\frac{2\MPartiii(s)}s.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Superpolynomial moment decay}

\begin{lemma}\label{p10:lem:moment-decay}
For every \(N>0\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:moment-decay}
 m_k=O_N(k^{-N})\qquad(k\to\infty).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For \(k>0\), the tail formula and symmetry give
\[
 m_k=k\int_0^1x^{k-1}(1-\F(x))\dd x
 =k\int_0^1x^{k-1}\F(1-x)\dd x.
\]
By \cref{p10:eq:F-flat-bound}, \(\F(1-x)\le C_N(1-x)^N\), hence
\[
 m_k\le C_N kB(k,N+1)=O(k^{-N}).
\]
\end{proof}

\subsection{Two globally convergent Newton series}

\begin{theorem}[Newton and central-even series]\label{p10:thm:newton}
Both of the following series converge locally uniformly for all \(s\in\C\):
\begin{align}
 \MPartiii(s)&=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^k\binom{s}{k}m_k,
 \label{p10:eq:M-Newton}\\
 \MPartiii(s)&=2^{-s}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\binom{s}{2j}\mu_{2j}
 =2^{-s}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\binom{s}{2j}\frac{2m_{2j+1}}{2j+1}.
 \label{p10:eq:M-central}
\end{align}
They therefore represent the entire function \(\MPartiii\) on the whole plane.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For \(0<Y<1\), expand
\(Y^s=(1-(1-Y))^s\).  Taking expectations and using
\(1-Y\overset d=Y\) gives \cref{p10:eq:M-Newton}.  On every compact set in \(s\), the
binomial coefficients have at most polynomial growth in \(k\); \cref{p10:lem:moment-decay}
therefore justifies absolute, locally uniform convergence and interchange of sum and
expectation.

For \cref{p10:eq:M-central}, write \(Y=(1+Z)/2\), expand \((1+Z)^s\), and use the
evenness of the law of \(Z\).  The relation \cref{p10:eq:mu-m-relation} and
\cref{p10:lem:moment-decay} again give locally uniform convergence.
\end{proof}

Several useful specializations are immediate.  For an integer \(r\ge1\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:negative-moment-rational-series}
 \MPartiii(-r)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{r+k-1}{k}m_k.
\end{equation}
Every term is rational and positive.  In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Mminus1-series}
 \MPartiii(-1)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}m_k.
\end{equation}
Differentiation of \cref{p10:eq:M-Newton} at the origin yields logarithmic moments in
terms of signed Stirling numbers of the first kind \(s(k,r)\):
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:log-moment-Stirling}
 \MPartiii^{(r)}(0)
 =r!\sum_{k=r}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^ks(k,r)}{k!}m_k.
\end{equation}
For \(r=1\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:log-moment-constant}
 -\E\log Y
 =\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{m_k}{k}
 =\int_0^1\frac{\F(x)}x\dd x.
\end{equation}
Numerically,
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:log-moment-numeric}
 -\E\log Y=0.758240000404408656\ldots.
\end{equation}

\subsection{A mixed reflected Mellin function}

The inverse symmetry \cref{p10:eq:Qsymmetry} suggests the two-variable transform
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:M2def}
 \MPartiii_2(a,b)=\int_0^1\Q(v)^a\Q(1-v)^b\dd v
 =\E\bigl[Y^a(1-Y)^b\bigr].
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}\label{p10:prop:M2}
The function \(\MPartiii_2\) is entire on \(\C^2\), symmetric in \(a,b\), and has the
locally uniformly convergent expansion
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:M2-series}
 \MPartiii_2(a,b)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^k\binom bk\MPartiii(a+k).
\end{equation}
It also has the central expansion
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:M2-central}
 \MPartiii_2(a,b)=2^{-a-b}
 \sum_{j,k\ge0}(-1)^k\binom aj\binom bk\mu_{j+k}.
\end{equation}
If either parameter is a nonnegative integer, the corresponding binomial expansion
terminates.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Endpoint flatness at both \(0\) and \(1\) gives local domination for arbitrary negative
real parts, proving entire dependence on two parameters.  Expand \((1-Y)^b\) to obtain
\cref{p10:eq:M2-series}; superpolynomial decay of shifted moments gives local uniformity.
The central formula follows from \(Y=(1+Z)/2\), \(1-Y=(1-Z)/2\).
\end{proof}

For example,
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:M2-examples}
 \int_0^1\Q(v)\Q(1-v)\dd v=\frac29,
 \qquad
 \int_0^1\Q(v)^2\Q(1-v)^2\dd v=\frac{34}{675},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:M2-negative-example}
 \int_0^1\frac{\dd v}{\Q(v)(1-\Q(v))}=2\MPartiii(-1).
\end{equation}

\section{Negative moments: an exact recurrence and a new asymptotic constant}

The Newton series \cref{p10:eq:negative-moment-rational-series} is effective for moderate
negative orders.  A second construction is much more stable for large orders and reveals
a new normalization law.

\subsection{The recurrence}

For \(s>0\), define
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:A-D-def}
 A(s)=\MPartiii(-s)=\E Y^{-s},
 \qquad
 D(s)=\E(1+Y)^{-s}.
\end{equation}
Conditioning in \cref{p10:eq:smoothing} and integrating the uniform variable gives the
following exact relation.

\begin{theorem}[negative-moment recurrence]\label{p10:thm:negative-recurrence}
For every real \(s>0\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:negative-recurrence}
 A(s+1)=\frac{2^{s+1}}s\bigl(A(s)-D(s)\bigr).
\end{equation}
Equivalently, for \(s>1\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:negative-recurrence-shifted}
 A(s)=\frac{2^s}{s-1}\bigl(A(s-1)-D(s-1)\bigr).
\end{equation}
Moreover \(D(s)\) has the rapidly convergent centered series
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:D-centered-series}
 D(s)=\left(\frac23\right)^s
 \sum_{j=0}^{\infty}
 \frac{(s)_{2j}}{(2j)!\,3^{2j}}\mu_{2j},
\end{equation}
where \((s)_n=s(s+1)\cdots(s+n-1)\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By \cref{p10:eq:smoothing}, for \(s>0\),
\[
 A(s+1)=2^{s+1}\E\int_0^1(U+Y')^{-s-1}\dd U.
\]
For \(y>0\),
\[
 \int_0^1(u+y)^{-s-1}\dd u
 =\frac{y^{-s}-(1+y)^{-s}}s,
\]
which gives \cref{p10:eq:negative-recurrence}.  For \cref{p10:eq:D-centered-series}, write
\(1+Y=(3+Z)/2\), expand \((1+Z/3)^{-s}\), and use the vanishing of odd moments.
The factor \(3^{-2j}\) makes the series geometrically stable in addition to the
superpolynomial decay of \(\mu_{2j}\).
\end{proof}

The first values generated by this recurrence are
\begin{align}
 A(1)&=2.31488614400581905\ldots,\nonumber\\
 A(2)&=6.55899183845822858\ldots,\nonumber\\
 A(3)&=24.3890791927221220\ldots,\nonumber\\
 A(4)&=128.368907270620532\ldots,\nonumber\\
 A(5)&=1025.15473735960333\ldots.\label{p10:eq:negative-values}
\end{align}

\subsection{A normalized limit and a periodic phase profile}

Define
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:H-def}
 H(s)=\frac{\Gamma(s)A(s)}{2^{s(s+1)/2}},
 \qquad
 \rho(s)=\frac{D(s)}{A(s)}.
\end{equation}
Then \cref{p10:eq:negative-recurrence} becomes
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:H-recurrence}
 H(s+1)=H(s)(1-\rho(s)).
\end{equation}
Both factors are positive.  Since \(\Prob(Y\le1/2)=1/2\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:rho-bound}
 A(s)\ge2^{s-1},\qquad D(s)\le1,
 \qquad 0<\rho(s)\le2^{1-s}.
\end{equation}
This elementary estimate is enough to prove convergence.

\begin{theorem}[periodic limiting profile]\label{p10:thm:phase-limit}
For \(0<\theta\le1\), the product
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Ctheta-product}
 C(\theta)=H(\theta)
 \prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\bigl(1-\rho(\theta+j)\bigr)
\end{equation}
converges to a positive number.  It extends by \(C(\theta+1)=C(\theta)\) to a
positive, continuous, one-periodic function.  As \(n\to\infty\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:A-phase-asymptotic}
 A(n+\theta)=C(\theta)
 \frac{2^{(n+\theta)(n+\theta+1)/2}}{\Gamma(n+\theta)}
 \bigl(1+O_\theta(2^{-n})\bigr).
\end{equation}
For integer \(r\to\infty\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:integer-negative-asymptotic}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \MPartiii(-r)=C_*\frac{2^{r(r+1)/2}}{\Gamma(r)}
 \bigl(1+O(2^{-r})\bigr),}
 \qquad C_*=C(1)>0.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Iteration of \cref{p10:eq:H-recurrence} gives
\[
 H(n+\theta)=H(\theta)
 \prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(1-\rho(\theta+j)).
\]
The sum of the tail bounds in \cref{p10:eq:rho-bound} is geometric.  Hence the infinite
product converges to a positive limit, and its omitted tail differs from \(1\) by
\(O_\theta(2^{-n})\).  This proves \cref{p10:eq:A-phase-asymptotic}.  Shifting the
initial point by one and using \cref{p10:eq:H-recurrence} proves exact periodicity.
Uniform convergence on compact subintervals gives continuity; exact periodicity
then removes the apparent endpoint at \(\theta=0\).
The integer formula is the case \(\theta=1\).
\end{proof}

For integers the normalized sequence
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Cr-def}
 C_r=\frac{(r-1)!\,\MPartiii(-r)}{2^{r(r+1)/2}}
\end{equation}
is strictly decreasing and satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Cr-rec}
 C_{r+1}=C_r\left(1-\frac{D(r)}{A(r)}\right),
 \qquad C_r\downarrow C_*.
\end{equation}
The product representation is
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Cstar-product}
 C_*=\frac{\MPartiii(-1)}2
 \prod_{j=1}^{\infty}
 \left(1-\frac{D(j)}{A(j)}\right).
\end{equation}
The computation described in \cref{p10:sec:numerics} gives
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Cstar-numeric}
 C_*=0.750721980407870959\ldots.
\end{equation}
The rapid stabilization is visible in
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}rcccccc@{}}
\toprule
\(r\)&1&2&4&6&8&12\\
\midrule
\(C_r\)&1.15744307&0.81987398&0.75216157&0.75072879&
0.75072199&0.7507219804\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
