\subsection{Logarithmic asymptotics and entire-function growth}

Stirling's expansion in \cref{p10:eq:integer-negative-asymptotic} gives a complete
algebraic asymptotic series:
\begin{align}
 \log\MPartiii(-r)
 ={}&\frac{\log2}{2}r^2-r\log r
 +\left(1+\frac{\log2}{2}\right)r
 +\frac12\log r
 +\log\frac{C_*}{\sqrt{2\pi}}
 \nonumber\\
 &-\frac1{12r}+\frac1{360r^3}-\frac1{1260r^5}+\cdots,
 \label{p10:eq:log-negative-asymptotic}
\end{align}
where
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:log-Cstar-numeric}
 \log\frac{C_*}{\sqrt{2\pi}}
 =-1.20565842815389633\ldots.
\end{equation}
This refines the growth one would obtain by a direct saddle-point transfer from the
Lambert--\(W\) endpoint expansion.

There is also a clean entire-function consequence.  If
\(M_{\MPartiii}(R)=\max_{|s|=R}|\MPartiii(s)|\), then
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:maxmod-equality}
 |\MPartiii(s)|\le\E Y^{\Re s}\le\E Y^{-R}=\MPartiii(-R),
 \qquad |s|=R,
\end{equation}
while equality occurs at \(s=-R\).  Thus
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:maxmod-exact}
 M_{\MPartiii}(R)=\MPartiii(-R).
\end{equation}

\begin{corollary}\label{p10:cor:order-type}
The entire function \(\MPartiii\) has order \(2\) and type \((\log2)/2\).
\end{corollary}

\subsection{Singular Fabius integrals}

Putting \(s=p+1\) in \cref{p10:eq:Mellin-F} shows that
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:weighted-F-complete}
 \int_0^1x^p\F(x)\dd x
 =\frac{1-\MPartiii(p+1)}{p+1}
\end{equation}
is an entire function of \(p\), despite the apparent pole at \(p=-1\).  Therefore
all negative powers are integrable against \(\F\).  For integers \(k\ge2\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:singular-F-integers}
 \int_0^1\frac{\F(x)}{x^k}\dd x
 =\frac{\MPartiii(1-k)-1}{k-1}.
\end{equation}
The first examples are
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1\frac{\F(x)}{x}\dd x
 &=0.758240000404408656\ldots,\nonumber\\
 \int_0^1\frac{\F(x)}{x^2}\dd x
 &=1.31488614400581905\ldots,\nonumber\\
 \int_0^1\frac{\F(x)}{x^3}\dd x
 &=2.77949591922911429\ldots,\nonumber\\
 \int_0^1\frac{\F(x)}{x^4}\dd x
 &=7.79635973090737400\ldots.\label{p10:eq:singular-F-values}
\end{align}
By \cref{p10:eq:integer-negative-asymptotic},
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:singular-F-asymptotic}
 \int_0^1\frac{\F(x)}{x^k}\dd x
 \sim C_*\frac{2^{k(k-1)/2}}{\Gamma(k)}.
\end{equation}
This is an asymptotic law for a natural family of increasingly singular antiderivatives.

\section{Definite weighted integrals}

\subsection{Polynomial and analytic weights}

Let \(g\) be integrable and let \(G(x)=\int_0^xg(t)\dd t\).  Taking \(A=G\),
\(B(v)=v\) in \cref{p10:thm:AB} gives
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1g(x)\F(x)\dd x&=G(1)-\E G(Y),
 \label{p10:eq:analytic-kernel-F}\\
 \int_0^1g(x)(1-\F(x))\dd x&=\E G(Y).
 \label{p10:eq:analytic-kernel-up}
\end{align}
For a polynomial \(g\), every quantity on the right is a rational combination of the
moments \(m_n\).  For an entire \(g\), the same formulas give convergent series whenever
the Taylor expansion can be integrated termwise.

The monomial cases are
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1x^p\F(x)\dd x&=\frac{1-\MPartiii(p+1)}{p+1},
 \label{p10:eq:monomial-F-definite}\\
 \int_0^1x^p\upf(x)\dd x&=\frac{\MPartiii(p+1)}{p+1}
 \qquad(\upf=1-\F\text{ on }[0,1]).
 \label{p10:eq:monomial-up-definite}
\end{align}
For \(p\in\N\) these are rational.  For example,
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}c|cccc@{}}
\toprule
\(p\)&0&1&2&3\\
\midrule
\(\int_0^1x^p\F(x)\dd x\)&\(1/2\)&\(13/36\)&\(5/18\)&\(1207/5400\)\\
\(\int_0^1x^p\upf(x)\dd x\)&\(1/2\)&\(5/36\)&\(1/18\)&\(143/5400\)\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

For symmetric weights \(g(x)=g(1-x)\), symmetry alone gives
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:symmetric-weight}
 \int_0^1g(x)\F(x)\dd x=\frac12\int_0^1g(x)\dd x.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Bernoulli-polynomial weights}

Let
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:beta-n-def}
 \beta_n=\E B_n(Y).
\end{equation}
Their exponential generating function follows from \cref{p10:eq:P-product}:
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:beta-egf}
 \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\beta_n\frac{t^n}{n!}
 =\frac{tP(t)}{e^t-1}.
\end{equation}
Symmetry implies \(\beta_{2j+1}=0\).  Since
\(B'_{n+1}=(n+1)B_n\), \cref{p10:eq:analytic-kernel-F} gives, for \(n\ge1\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Bernoulli-F-integral}
 \int_0^1B_n(x)\F(x)\dd x
 =-\frac{\beta_{n+1}-B_{n+1}}{n+1}.
\end{equation}
The first nontrivial expected Bernoulli values are
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:beta-values}
 \beta_2=-\frac1{18},\quad
 \beta_4=\frac{23}{1350},\quad
 \beta_6=-\frac{1543}{119070},\quad
 \beta_8=\frac{1196113}{65063250}.
\end{equation}
This supplies a direct integral bridge between the Bernoulli-polynomial structure and
the Fabius distribution.

\subsection{Beta weights}

For \(\Re a,\Re b>0\), define
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:IFab}
 I_F(a,b)=\int_0^1x^{a-1}(1-x)^{b-1}\F(x)\dd x.
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[beta-weighted Fabius integrals]\label{p10:thm:beta-F}
For \(\Re a,\Re b>0\),
\begin{align}
 I_F(a,b)
 &=\E B_Y(b,a)
 =\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}
 \frac{(1-a)_n}{n!(b+n)}\MPartiii(b+n),
 \label{p10:eq:beta-F-series}\\
 \int_0^1x^{a-1}(1-x)^{b-1}(1-\F(x))\dd x
 &=\E B_Y(a,b)
 =\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}
 \frac{(1-b)_n}{n!(a+n)}\MPartiii(a+n).
 \label{p10:eq:beta-up-series}
\end{align}
Here \(B_y\) is the incomplete beta function.  If \(a\) in
\cref{p10:eq:beta-F-series}, or \(b\) in \cref{p10:eq:beta-up-series}, is a positive integer,
the corresponding series terminates.  If both parameters are positive integers, the
integrals are rational.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use \(\F(x)=\E\ind_{\{Y\le x\}}\) and Fubini:
\[
 I_F(a,b)=\E\int_Y^1x^{a-1}(1-x)^{b-1}\dd x
 =\E B_{1-Y}(b,a)=\E B_Y(b,a),
\]
where the last equality uses \(1-Y\overset d=Y\).  The hypergeometric expansion
\[
 B_y(b,a)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}
 \frac{(1-a)_n}{n!(b+n)}y^{b+n}
\]
gives \cref{p10:eq:beta-F-series}.  The endpoint flatness and
\cref{p10:lem:moment-decay} justify convergence beyond the ordinary boundary restrictions
of the pointwise hypergeometric series.  The second identity is analogous.
\end{proof}

For example, symmetry or direct termination gives
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:beta-example}
 \int_0^1x(1-x)\F(x)\dd x=\frac1{12}.
\end{equation}
Differentiating \cref{p10:eq:beta-F-series} in \(a\) and \(b\) produces all beta-logarithmic
integrals with powers of \(\log x\) and \(\log(1-x)\).

\subsection{A dyadic half-interval identity}

The lower-half differential equation \(\F'(x)=2\F(2x)\) for \(0\le x\le1/2\)
leads to exact quantile truncations.

\begin{proposition}\label{p10:prop:half-identities}
For every complex \(p\), with removable values interpreted by continuation,
\begin{align}
 \int_0^{1/2}\Q(v)^p\dd v
 &=2^{-p}\frac{1-\MPartiii(p+1)}{p+1},
 \label{p10:eq:half-Q}\\
 \int_0^{1/2}x^p\F(x)\dd x
 &=\frac{2^{-p-1}}{p+1}
 \left[\frac12-\frac{1-\MPartiii(p+2)}{p+2}\right].
 \label{p10:eq:half-F}
\end{align}
In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:half-F-zero}
 \int_0^{1/2}\F(x)\dd x=\frac5{72}.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Change variables \(v=\F(x)\) in the first integral and use the lower-half equation:
\[
 \int_0^{1/2}\Q(v)^p\dd v
 =\int_0^{1/2}x^p\F'(x)\dd x
 =2^{-p}\int_0^1y^p\F(y)\dd y.
\]
This is \cref{p10:eq:half-Q}.  Substitute it into the incomplete formula
\cref{p10:eq:monomial-F-antiderivative} at \(x=1/2\) to obtain \cref{p10:eq:half-F}.
\end{proof}

\section{Fractional and integer antiderivatives}

Repeated integration has a particularly transparent probabilistic meaning.  The same
calculus extends without change from integer order to the Riemann--Liouville operator.
For \(\Re\alpha>0\), write
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:RLdef}
 (I_0^\alpha f)(x)
 =\frac1{\Gamma(\alpha)}\int_0^x(x-t)^{\alpha-1}f(t)\dd t.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Fractional inverse-pair duality}

\begin{theorem}[fractional power duality]\label{p10:thm:fractional-duality}
Let \(\Re\alpha>0\).  Initially for \(\Re q>0\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:RL-Fq}
 (I_0^\alpha\F^q)(x)
 =\frac{q}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}
 \int_0^{\F(x)}(x-\Q(v))^\alpha v^{q-1}\dd v.
\end{equation}
Initially for \(\Re p>0\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:RL-Qp}
 (I_0^\alpha\Q^p)(v)
 =\frac{p}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}
 \int_0^{\Q(v)}(v-\F(x))^\alpha x^{p-1}\dd x.
\end{equation}
Both formulas continue in \(p\) or \(q\) wherever the endpoint terms admit continuation.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since \(\dd(x-t)^\alpha=-\alpha(x-t)^{\alpha-1}\dd t\), integration by parts gives
\[
 I_0^\alpha\F^q(x)
 =\frac1{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}
 \int_0^x(x-t)^\alpha\dd(\F(t)^q).
\]
The boundary terms vanish when \(\Re q>0\).  Using
\(\dd(\F^q)=q\F^{q-1}\dd\F\) and substituting \(v=\F(t)\) proves
\cref{p10:eq:RL-Fq}.  The proof of \cref{p10:eq:RL-Qp} is identical after interchanging the
inverse pair.
\end{proof}

The case \(q=1\) has a direct truncated-moment interpretation.

\begin{corollary}\label{p10:cor:RL-F-prob}
For \(\Re\alpha>0\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:RL-F-prob}
 (I_0^\alpha\F)(x)
 =\frac{\E[(x-Y)_+^\alpha]}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}.
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:RL-F-at-one}
 (I_0^\alpha\F)(1)
 =\frac{\MPartiii(\alpha)}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}.
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
In \cref{p10:eq:RL-Fq}, the integral over \(v\) is the expectation of
\((x-\Q(V))_+^\alpha\) for a uniform \(V\).  Since \(\Q(V)\overset d=Y\), this is
\cref{p10:eq:RL-F-prob}.  At \(x=1\), use \(1-Y\overset d=Y\).
\end{proof}

Formula \cref{p10:eq:RL-F-at-one} says that the entire complex moment function is exactly
the endpoint value of the fractional antiderivative, multiplied by
\(\Gamma(\alpha+1)\).  Thus the Newton series in \cref{p10:eq:M-Newton} is at the same
time a global series for fractional repeated integration.

\subsection{Repeated primitives of Rvachev's up function}

For \(\Re\alpha>0\), define the left-anchored fractional integral
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:RL-up-def}
 (I_{-1}^\alpha\upf)(x)
 =\frac1{\Gamma(\alpha)}\int_{-1}^x(x-t)^{\alpha-1}\upf(t)\dd t.
\end{equation}
Because \(\upf\) is the density of \(Z\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:RL-up-prob}
 (I_{-1}^\alpha\upf)(x)
 =\frac{\E[(x-Z)_+^{\alpha-1}]}{\Gamma(\alpha)}.
\end{equation}
At the right endpoint,
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:RL-up-at-one}
 (I_{-1}^\alpha\upf)(1)
 =\frac{2^{\alpha-1}\MPartiii(\alpha-1)}{\Gamma(\alpha)}.
\end{equation}
For an integer \(n\ge1\), \cref{p10:eq:RL-up-prob} becomes
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:nth-up-primitive}
 (I_{-1}^n\upf)(x)
 =\frac1{(n-1)!}\E[(x-Z)_+^{n-1}].
\end{equation}

\subsection{Appell polynomial tails}

For \(x\ge1\), the positive-part signs in \cref{p10:eq:RL-F-prob,p10:eq:nth-up-primitive}
disappear.  Hence
\begin{align}
 (I_0^n\F)(x)&=\frac1{n!}\E(x-Y)^n,\qquad x\ge1,
 \label{p10:eq:F-Appell-tail}\\
 (I_{-1}^n\upf)(x)&=\frac1{(n-1)!}\E(x-Z)^{n-1},\qquad x\ge1.
 \label{p10:eq:up-Appell-tail}
\end{align}
The polynomial sequences
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Appell-polynomials}
 P_n(x)=\E(x-Y)^n,
 \qquad R_n(x)=\E(x-Z)^n
\end{equation}
are Appell sequences:
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Appell-derivative}
 P_n'(x)=nP_{n-1}(x),\qquad R_n'(x)=nR_{n-1}(x).
\end{equation}
Their exponential generating functions are
\begin{align}
 \sum_{n\ge0}P_n(x)\frac{t^n}{n!}
 &=e^{tx}P(-t),\label{p10:eq:Pn-egf}\\
 \sum_{n\ge0}R_n(x)\frac{t^n}{n!}
 &=e^{tx}\mathcal U(-t).\label{p10:eq:Rn-egf}
\end{align}
These polynomial tails and their inverse-moment projection role are part of the existing
Rvachev--Appell research in the repository; here they arise as the exterior part of the
fractional-truncated-moment formula.

\subsection{Ordinary \(n\)-fold antiderivatives as incomplete moments}

For an integer \(n\ge1\), the anchored \(n\)-fold antiderivative of \(\F\) is
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:nth-F-primitive}
 I_0^n\F(x)=\frac1{n!}
 \int_0^{\F(x)}(x-\Q(v))^n\dd v.
\end{equation}
The same expression can be expanded finitely:
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:nth-F-incomplete-moments}
 I_0^n\F(x)
 =\frac1{n!}\sum_{j=0}^n(-1)^j\binom njx^{n-j}
 \int_0^{\F(x)}\Q(v)^j\dd v.
\end{equation}
Thus every repeated primitive is determined by incomplete quantile moments.  At a dyadic
\(x\), these can in turn be reduced by the exact dyadic machinery already developed in
the repository.

For inverse powers, \cref{p10:eq:RL-Qp} gives the complementary expression
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:nth-Q-primitive}
 I_0^n(\Q^p)(v)
 =\frac{p}{n!}\int_0^{\Q(v)}x^{p-1}(v-\F(x))^n\dd x.
\end{equation}
The special case \(p=1\) is a fractional extension of the area identity:
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:nth-Q-area}
 I_0^n\Q(v)=\frac1{n!}\int_0^{\Q(v)}(v-\F(x))^n\dd x.
\end{equation}
For \(n=1\), this is
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:classic-inverse-area}
 \int_0^v\Q(w)\dd w=\int_0^{\Q(v)}(v-\F(x))\dd x,
\end{equation}
the usual area formula in a form adapted to the Fabius quantile.

\section{Derivatives of arbitrary order}

\subsection{Derivatives of the Fabius function}

On the lower dyadic branch, repeated differentiation of
\(\F'(x)=2\F(2x)\) gives
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:F-nth-lower}
 \F^{(n)}(x)=2^{n(n+1)/2}\F(2^nx),
 \qquad 0<x<2^{-n}.
\end{equation}
The full interval is obtained by the known dyadic/Thue--Morse reflection formulas.
We do not duplicate those formulas here; the role of \cref{p10:eq:F-nth-lower} is to show
why every inverse derivative becomes extremely singular near the endpoints.

\subsection{Inverse derivatives and Bell polynomials}

Let \(f_j=\F^{(j)}(x)\).  Define rational differential expressions recursively by
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Rinverse-rec}
 \mathcal R_1(x)=\frac1{\F'(x)},
 \qquad
 \mathcal R_{n+1}(x)=\frac1{\F'(x)}\frac{\dd}{\dd x}\mathcal R_n(x).
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[all inverse derivatives]\label{p10:prop:inverse-derivatives}
For \(0<v<1\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Q-nth-operator}
 \Q^{(n)}(v)=\mathcal R_n(\Q(v)).
\end{equation}
Equivalently, for \(n\ge2\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Q-Bell-rec}
 \Q^{(n)}(v)
 =-\frac1{\F'(\Q(v))}
 \sum_{k=2}^n\F^{(k)}(\Q(v))
 B_{n,k}\bigl(\Q'(v),\ldots,\Q^{(n-k+1)}(v)\bigr),
\end{equation}
where \(B_{n,k}\) is the partial exponential Bell polynomial.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The operator relation follows by applying \(\dd/\dd v=(\F'(x))^{-1}\dd/\dd x\) at
\(x=\Q(v)\).  The Bell recurrence follows by differentiating
\(\F(\Q(v))=v\) \(n\) times and isolating the term containing \(\Q^{(n)}\).
\end{proof}

The first expressions are
\begin{align}
 \Q'&=\frac1{f_1},\nonumber\\
 \Q''&=-\frac{f_2}{f_1^3},\nonumber\\
 \Q'''&=\frac{3f_2^2-f_1f_3}{f_1^5},\nonumber\\
 \Q''''&=\frac{-15f_2^3+10f_1f_2f_3-f_1^2f_4}{f_1^7},
 \label{p10:eq:Q-first-derivatives}
\end{align}
with every \(f_j\) evaluated at \(x=\Q(v)\).  Combining these formulas with the
Lambert--\(W\) inverse endpoint expansion from the repository gives asymptotics of any
fixed derivative order.

\subsection{Derivatives of integral transforms}

Since \(\MPartiii\) is entire,
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:M-nth-derivative}
 \MPartiii^{(n)}(s)=\int_0^1\Q(v)^s(\log\Q(v))^n\dd v.
\end{equation}
The bilateral up transform satisfies, away from its complex zeros,
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:U-Bell-derivative}
 \mathcal U^{(n)}(t)
 =\mathcal U(t)\,
 B_n\bigl(L_1(t),\ldots,L_n(t)\bigr),
\end{equation}
where \(B_n\) is the complete exponential Bell polynomial and
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Lj-def}
 L_j(t)=\frac{\dd^j}{\dd t^j}\log\mathcal U(t)
 =\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-kj}
 \ell^{(j)}(t/2^k),
 \qquad \ell(z)=\log\frac{\sinh z}{z}.
\end{equation}
At \(t=0\), these are precisely the cumulants in \cref{p10:eq:Z-cumulants}; the
continuation of \cref{p10:eq:U-Bell-derivative} through zeros is obtained by cancelling the
apparent logarithmic singularities.

\section{Endpoint fractional calculus of the inverse Fabius function}
\label{p10:sec:inverse-fractional-endpoint}

The inverse function is the most singular object in the Fabius--Rvachev system.
Ordinary derivatives of \(\Q\) blow up at both endpoints, while \(\Q\) itself tends to
zero more slowly than every positive power.  This combination is exactly the regime in
which fractional differentiation has a sharp critical exponent.  The results in this
section are not contained in the audited corpus: the repository develops the
Lambert--\(W\) inversion and integer inverse derivatives, but not the fractional
endpoint law or its strong critical \(L^p\) inclusion.

Put
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:T-eta-def}
 T=\log\frac1y,
 \qquad \ell=\log2,
 \qquad
 \eta(y)=\frac{y\Q'(y)}{\Q(y)}.
\end{equation}
The dimensionless quantity \(\eta\) is the logarithmic elasticity of the quantile.  It
is the correct small parameter: powers of \(1/T\) alone miss the square-root scale and
the repository's periodic Lambert phase.

\begin{proposition}[quantile scale and slow variation]\label{p10:prop:Q-slow-variation}
As \(y\downarrow0\),
\begin{align}
 \Q(y)
 &=\frac{\sqrt T}{e\sqrt\ell}
   \exp\!\bigl(-\sqrt{2\ell T}\bigr)
   \left(1+O\!\left(\frac{\log T}{\sqrt T}\right)\right),
 \label{p10:eq:Q-leading}\\
 \eta(y)
 &=\sqrt{\frac{\ell}{2T}}
   +O\!\left(\frac{\log T}{T}\right).
 \label{p10:eq:eta-leading}
\end{align}
For each fixed \(c>0\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Q-slow}
 \frac{\Q(cy)}{\Q(y)}\longrightarrow1.
\end{equation}
Thus \(\Q\), and every positive power \(\Q^\beta\), is slowly varying at the
origin in the multiplicative sense.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Equation \eqref{p10:eq:Q-leading} is the leading term of the repository's corrected
Lambert--\(W\) inverse expansion.  The full expansion is differentiable term by term;
its logarithmic derivative gives \eqref{p10:eq:eta-leading}.  Alternatively, the leading
term contributes
\(
 \sqrt{\ell/(2T)}-1/(2T)
\)
to \(y\Q'/\Q\), while differentiating the smooth periodic correction contributes
\(O((\log T)/T)\).  Replacing \(y\) by \(cy\) changes \(T\) by the bounded number
\(-\log c\); the difference of the square roots in \eqref{p10:eq:Q-leading} therefore tends
to zero.  This proves \eqref{p10:eq:Q-slow}.
\end{proof}

For \(a>0\), let
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:continuous-harmonic}
 H_a=\psi(a+1)+\gamma,
\end{equation}
where \(\psi\) is the digamma function and \(\gamma\) is Euler's constant.  Thus
\(H_n=1+1/2+\cdots+1/n\) when \(n\) is a positive integer.

\begin{theorem}[digamma correction for fractional quantile primitives]
\label{p10:thm:Q-fractional-primitive-asymptotic}
Let \(a>0\) and \(\beta>0\).  Then
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Q-frac-primitive-eta}
 I_{0+}^{a}(\Q^\beta)(y)
 =\frac{y^a\Q(y)^\beta}{\Gamma(a+1)}
  \left(1-\beta H_a\eta(y)+o(\eta(y))\right),
 \qquad y\downarrow0.
\end{equation}
Using \eqref{p10:eq:eta-leading},
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Q-frac-primitive-explicit}
 I_{0+}^{a}(\Q^\beta)(y)
 =\frac{y^a\Q(y)^\beta}{\Gamma(a+1)}
 \left[
  1-\beta H_a\sqrt{\frac{\ell}{2T}}
  +O\!\left(\frac{\log T}{T}\right)
 \right].
\end{equation}
For \(a=n\in\N\), this gives the first nontrivial endpoint correction to every
\(n\)-fold antiderivative of every positive quantile power.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
After \(v=yu\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Q-frac-beta-kernel}
 I_{0+}^{a}(\Q^\beta)(y)
 =\frac{y^a\Q(y)^\beta}{\Gamma(a)}
  \int_0^1(1-u)^{a-1}
       \left(\frac{\Q(yu)}{\Q(y)}\right)^\beta\dd u.
\end{equation}
The differentiated endpoint expansion gives, uniformly on compact logarithmic
\(u\)-ranges,
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Q-ratio-local}
 \log\frac{\Q(yu)}{\Q(y)}
 =\eta(y)\log u+o(\eta(y))(1+|\log u|).
\end{equation}
Potter bounds for slowly varying functions dominate the remaining small-\(u\) tail by
an integrable power.  We may therefore expand the exponential in
\eqref{p10:eq:Q-ratio-local} under the beta integral.  If \(U_a\) has density
\(a(1-u)^{a-1}\) on \((0,1)\), then
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:beta-log-moment}
 \E\log U_a=\psi(1)-\psi(a+1)=-H_a.
\end{equation}
The constant term in \eqref{p10:eq:Q-frac-beta-kernel} is
\(1/\Gamma(a+1)\); the linear term is \(-\beta H_a\eta(y)\).  This proves
\eqref{p10:eq:Q-frac-primitive-eta}, and \eqref{p10:eq:eta-leading} gives
\eqref{p10:eq:Q-frac-primitive-explicit}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[second endpoint correction]\label{p10:rem:Q-second-correction}
Let \(\delta(y)=y\eta'(y)\), and put
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Va}
 V_a=H_a^2+\psi_1(1)-\psi_1(a+1),
\end{equation}
where \(\psi_1\) is the trigamma function.  Expanding the logarithmic endpoint jet one
order further gives the formal, and under the repository's all-orders smooth expansion
rigorous, refinement
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Q-second-correction}
 \frac{\Gamma(a+1)I_{0+}^{a}(\Q^\beta)(y)}{y^a\Q(y)^\beta}
 =1-\beta H_a\eta
 +\frac{V_a}{2}\bigl(\beta^2\eta^2+\beta\delta\bigr)
 +o(\eta^2+|\delta|).
\end{equation}
The appearance of digamma and trigamma values is forced by the first two logarithmic
moments of a \(\mathrm{Beta}(1,a)\) variable.  At integer \(a\), these reduce to
harmonic and second-order harmonic numbers, linking the fractional expansion directly
to the Bernoulli/polygamma organization already present in the repository.
\end{remark}

For \(0<\sigma<1\), define
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Ksigma}
 K_\sigma=\psi(1)-\psi(1-\sigma)
          =-\gamma-\psi(1-\sigma)>0.
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[fractional inverse derivative and the sharp critical exponent]
\label{p10:thm:Q-fractional-derivative-Lp}
Let \(0<\sigma<1\) and \(\beta>0\).  Since \(\Q(0)=0\), the left Caputo and
Riemann--Liouville derivatives of \(\Q^\beta\) coincide.  As \(y\downarrow0\),
\begin{align}
 D_{0+}^{\sigma}(\Q^\beta)(y)
 &=\frac{y^{-\sigma}\Q(y)^\beta}{\Gamma(1-\sigma)}
   \left(1+\beta K_\sigma\eta(y)+o(\eta(y))\right)
 \label{p10:eq:Q-frac-derivative-eta}\\
 &=\frac{y^{-\sigma}\Q(y)^\beta}{\Gamma(1-\sigma)}
   \left[
    1+\beta K_\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\ell}{2T}}
    +O\!\left(\frac{\log T}{T}\right)
   \right].
 \label{p10:eq:Q-frac-derivative-explicit}
\end{align}
Consequently, for every \(0<p<\infty\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Q-frac-Lp-threshold}
 D_{0+}^{\sigma}(\Q^\beta)\in L^p(0,\tfrac12)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \sigma p\le1.
\end{equation}
By reflection, the same criterion holds for the right derivative
\(D_{1-}^{\sigma}(1-\Q)^\beta\) on \((1/2,1)\).  In particular, the critical
exponent \(p=1/\sigma\) is included in the strong \(L^p\) space, not merely in weak
\(L^p\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{p10:thm:Q-fractional-primitive-asymptotic} with \(a=1-\sigma\) and
differentiate.  Equivalently, compare with the exact model
\(
 D_{0+}^{\sigma}y^\rho
 =\Gamma(1+\rho)y^{\rho-\sigma}/\Gamma(1+\rho-\sigma)
\)
and let the local logarithmic exponent \(\rho\) tend to \(\beta\eta(y)\).  The
linear coefficient is
\(
 \psi(1)-\psi(1-\sigma)=K_\sigma
\), which proves \eqref{p10:eq:Q-frac-derivative-eta}.  The endpoint expansion gives
\eqref{p10:eq:Q-frac-derivative-explicit}.

It remains to decide integrability.  The leading asymptotic reduces the question to
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:critical-integral}
 \int_0^\varepsilon y^{-\sigma p}\Q(y)^{\beta p}\dd y.
\end{equation}
Set \(y=e^{-T}\).  By \eqref{p10:eq:Q-leading}, the logarithm of the transformed
integrand, including \(\dd y=e^{-T}\dd T\), is
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:critical-exponent}
 (\sigma p-1)T-\beta p\sqrt{2\ell T}+O(\log T).
\end{equation}
It tends linearly to \(-\infty\) if \(\sigma p<1\), linearly to \(+\infty\) if
\(\sigma p>1\), and at \(\sigma p=1\) retains the integrable stretched-exponential
factor \(\exp(-\beta p\sqrt{2\ell T}+O(\log T))\).  This proves
\eqref{p10:eq:Q-frac-Lp-threshold}.  Reflection follows from
\(1-\Q(1-y)=\Q(y)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[integer endpoint as a limit]\label{p10:cor:Qprime-critical}
The limiting case \(\sigma\uparrow1\) predicts and is consistent with
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Qprime-Lp}
 \Q'\in L^1(0,1),
 \qquad
 \Q'\notin L^p(0,1)\quad(p>1),
\end{equation}
which is the known sharp first-inverse-derivative threshold.  Thus
\eqref{p10:eq:Q-frac-Lp-threshold} is a genuine continuous-order interpolation of the
integer result.
\end{corollary}

\section{Nonlocal fractional calculus of Rvachev's up-function}
\label{p10:sec:up-nonlocal-fractional}

Riemann--Liouville operators are one-sided and adapted to antiderivatives.  The Riesz
potential and fractional Laplacian probe a different aspect: they are translation
invariant and nonlocal, so a compactly supported function acquires explicit algebraic
tails.  For \(\upf\), those tails are governed by rational moments and therefore by the
same Bell--Bernoulli arithmetic as the local theory.

For \(0<s<1\), use the one-dimensional normalization
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:fraclap-def}
 (-\Delta)^s f(x)
 =c_{1,s}\,\operatorname{PV}\!\int_{\R}
   \frac{f(x)-f(t)}{|x-t|^{1+2s}}\dd t,
 \qquad
 c_{1,s}=\frac{4^s\Gamma(\frac12+s)}{\sqrt\pi\,|\Gamma(-s)|}.
\end{equation}
For \(0<a<1\), define the Riesz potential
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Riesz-def}
 (\mathcal I_a f)(x)
 =d_{1,a}\int_{\R}|x-t|^{a-1}f(t)\dd t,
 \qquad
 d_{1,a}=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1-a}{2})}
 {2^a\sqrt\pi\,\Gamma(\frac a2)}.
\end{equation}
With the Fourier convention of this report, their multipliers are \(|\xi|^{2s}\)
and \(|\xi|^{-a}\), respectively.

\begin{theorem}[exact exterior series for the fractional Laplacian]
\label{p10:thm:up-fraclap-exterior}
Let \(0<s<1\) and \(|x|>1\).  Then
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:up-fraclap-exterior}
 \boxed{
 (-\Delta)^s\upf(x)
 =-c_{1,s}|x|^{-1-2s}
  \sum_{j=0}^{\infty}
   \frac{(1+2s)_{2j}}{(2j)!}
   \frac{\mu_{2j}}{|x|^{2j}}.}
\end{equation}
The series is absolutely and locally uniformly convergent on \(|x|>1\).  Its first
terms are
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:up-fraclap-first-terms}
 (-\Delta)^s\upf(x)
 =-c_{1,s}|x|^{-1-2s}
 \left[
  1+\frac{(1+2s)(1+s)}{9|x|^2}
  +\frac{19(1+2s)_4}{16200|x|^4}
  +\cdots
 \right].
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Outside the support, \(\upf(x)=0\), so \eqref{p10:eq:fraclap-def} becomes
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:fraclap-outside-integral}
 (-\Delta)^s\upf(x)
 =-c_{1,s}\int_{-1}^1\frac{\upf(t)}{|x-t|^{1+2s}}\dd t.
\end{equation}
For \(x>1\), expand
\(
 (x-t)^{-1-2s}
 =x^{-1-2s}\sum_{n\ge0}(1+2s)_n(t/x)^n/n!
\).
The expansion is uniformly absolutely convergent on every region \(x\ge1+\varepsilon\).
Evenness annihilates all odd moments and gives \eqref{p10:eq:up-fraclap-exterior}.  The
case \(x<-1\) is the same by symmetry.  Substituting
\(\mu_2=1/9\) and \(\mu_4=19/675\) gives
\eqref{p10:eq:up-fraclap-first-terms}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[exact exterior Riesz-potential series]
\label{p10:thm:up-Riesz-exterior}
Let \(0<a<1\) and \(|x|>1\).  Then
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:up-Riesz-exterior}
 \boxed{
 (\mathcal I_a\upf)(x)
 =d_{1,a}|x|^{a-1}
  \sum_{j=0}^{\infty}
   \frac{(1-a)_{2j}}{(2j)!}
   \frac{\mu_{2j}}{|x|^{2j}}.}
\end{equation}
Again the series is absolutely and locally uniformly convergent.  Thus every exterior
coefficient is an explicit gamma factor times a rational Fabius--Rvachev moment.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For \(x>1\), expand
\(
 (x-t)^{a-1}=x^{a-1}(1-t/x)^{-(1-a)}
\)
in \eqref{p10:eq:Riesz-def}; odd moments vanish.  Reflection gives \(x<-1\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Bell--Bernoulli generation of every coefficient]
\label{p10:prop:up-cumulants-nonlocal}
The even cumulants of the centered up law are
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:up-cumulants-nonlocal}
 \kappa_{2r}
 =\frac{2^{2r}B_{2r}}{2r(2^{2r}-1)},
 \qquad \kappa_{2r+1}=0.
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:up-moment-Bell}
 \mu_{2j}=B_{2j}(0,\kappa_2,0,\kappa_4,\ldots,0,\kappa_{2j}),
\end{equation}
where \(B_n\) is the complete exponential Bell polynomial.  Equations
\eqref{p10:eq:up-fraclap-exterior} and \eqref{p10:eq:up-Riesz-exterior} therefore give
closed Bernoulli--Bell series for both nonlocal operators.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Take the logarithm of
\begin{equation*}
 \mathcal U(z)=\prod_{k\ge1}\frac{\sinh(z/2^k)}{z/2^k}
\end{equation*}
and use
\begin{equation*}
 \log\!\left(\frac{\sinh z}{z}\right)
 =\sum_{r\ge1}\frac{2^{2r-1}B_{2r}}{r(2r)!}z^{2r}.
\end{equation*}
The geometric factor is
\(\sum_{k\ge1}2^{-2rk}=1/(2^{2r}-1)\), which gives
\eqref{p10:eq:up-cumulants-nonlocal}; the moment--cumulant relation is
\eqref{p10:eq:up-moment-Bell}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[fractional Sobolev energy as an infinite-sinc integral]
\label{p10:thm:up-fractional-energy}
For every \(s\ge0\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:up-fractional-energy}
 \mathcal E_s(\upf)
 :=\int_{\R}\upf(x)(-\Delta)^s\upf(x)\dd x
 =\frac1{2\pi}\int_{\R}|\xi|^{2s}
  \prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\sinc^2(\xi/2^k)\dd\xi.
\end{equation}
The integral is finite for every \(s\).  For \(0<s<1\), it also equals
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:up-Gagliardo-energy}
 \mathcal E_s(\upf)
 =\frac{c_{1,s}}2\iint_{\R^2}
  \frac{|\upf(x)-\upf(y)|^2}{|x-y|^{1+2s}}\dd x\dd y.
\end{equation}
Thus the full fractional Sobolev scale of \(\upf\) is encoded by one weighted family
of infinite sinc-square integrals.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Plancherel's theorem and the Fourier multiplier \(|\xi|^{2s}\) give
\eqref{p10:eq:up-fractional-energy}.  The up-function is \(C^\infty\) with compact support,
so its Fourier transform decays faster than every power and all energies are finite.
The standard symmetrization of the singular integral gives
\eqref{p10:eq:up-Gagliardo-energy}.
\end{proof}

\section{Fractional self-differential scaling and a dyadic Floquet corrector}
\label{p10:sec:Floquet-fractional}

The global signed self-differential extension \(\mathcal F\) satisfies
\(\mathcal F'(x)=2\mathcal F(2x)\).  Integer differentiation therefore stays on a
single dyadic dilation orbit.  The repository's Laplace analysis proves that standard
fractional differentiation does not: a nonconstant log-periodic phase is the exact
obstruction.  Here we retain that obstruction and turn it into a covariant correction.

Let
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:global-L-new}
 L(s)=\int_0^\infty e^{-sx}\mathcal F(x)\dd x,
 \qquad s>0.
\end{equation}
The dilation equation \(L(s/2)=sL(s)\) is equivalent to
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:global-L-Floquet}
 L(2^t)=2^{-t(t+1)/2}\Pi(t),
 \qquad \Pi(t+1)=\Pi(t),
\end{equation}
where \(\Pi\) is positive, nonconstant, and has minimal period one.  Define the
standard Laplace fractional derivative by
\(
 \mathcal L[\mathbf D^\alpha\mathcal F](s)=s^\alpha L(s)
\)
and the continuous dilation orbit by
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Talpha-new}
 (T_\alpha\mathcal F)(x)
 =2^{\alpha(\alpha+1)/2}\mathcal F(2^\alpha x).
\end{equation}
The known defect identity is
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:known-fractional-defect-new}
 \frac{\mathcal L[T_\alpha\mathcal F](s)}
      {\mathcal L[\mathbf D^\alpha\mathcal F](s)}
 =m_\alpha(s),
 \qquad
 m_\alpha(s)=\frac{\Pi(\log_2s-\alpha)}{\Pi(\log_2s)}.
\end{equation}
In particular, the ratio is identically one exactly for integer \(\alpha\).

\begin{theorem}[dyadic Floquet corrector and scale cocycle]
\label{p10:thm:Floquet-corrector}
Let \(\mathcal C_\alpha\) be the Laplace multiplier with symbol \(m_\alpha\):
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Calpha-def}
 \mathcal L[\mathcal C_\alpha f](s)=m_\alpha(s)\mathcal L[f](s).
\end{equation}
Then, on the cyclic vector \(\mathcal F\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:corrected-fractional-orbit}
 \boxed{
 T_\alpha\mathcal F
 =\mathcal C_\alpha\mathbf D^\alpha\mathcal F
 \qquad(\alpha\in\R).}
\end{equation}
The symbols satisfy the exact scale-cocycle law
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Floquet-cocycle}
 m_{\alpha+\beta}(s)
 =m_\alpha(s/2^\beta)m_\beta(s).
\end{equation}
Moreover \(m_n\equiv1\) for \(n\in\Z\), so the corrector disappears at every integer
order.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Equation \eqref{p10:eq:corrected-fractional-orbit} is
\eqref{p10:eq:known-fractional-defect-new} rewritten as an operator identity.  For the
cocycle,
\[
 m_\alpha(s/2^\beta)m_\beta(s)
 =\frac{\Pi(\log_2s-\beta-\alpha)}{\Pi(\log_2s-\beta)}
  \frac{\Pi(\log_2s-\beta)}{\Pi(\log_2s)}
 =m_{\alpha+\beta}(s).
\]
Period one gives \(m_n=1\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The corrected family is not an ordinary translation-invariant semigroup; the argument
shift \(s\mapsto s/2^\beta\) in \eqref{p10:eq:Floquet-cocycle} is essential.  It is better
viewed as a Floquet cocycle over logarithmic frequency.  This identifies a precise
candidate for a fractional calculus adapted to the Fabius dyadic orbit, rather than
forcing that orbit into the standard Riemann--Liouville semigroup.
\end{remark}


\section{Laplace, Fourier, Mellin, and resolvent transforms}

\subsection{One-sided Laplace transforms}

Integration by parts against the distribution measure \(\dd\F\) gives, for all
\(t\in\C\) with removable values at \(t=0\),
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1e^{tx}\F(x)\dd x
 &=\frac{e^t-P(t)}t,
 \label{p10:eq:Laplace-F}\\
 \int_0^1e^{tx}(1-\F(x))\dd x
 &=\frac{P(t)-1}t.
 \label{p10:eq:Laplace-up-half}
\end{align}
Consequently
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1x^ne^{tx}\F(x)\dd x
 &=\frac{\dd^n}{\dd t^n}\left(\frac{e^t-P(t)}t\right),
 \label{p10:eq:exp-poly-F}\\
 \int_0^1x^ne^{tx}\upf(x)\dd x
 &=\frac{\dd^n}{\dd t^n}\left(\frac{P(t)-1}t\right).
 \label{p10:eq:exp-poly-up}
\end{align}
Purely imaginary \(t\) yields the corresponding sine and cosine transforms.  On the
full line,
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:bilateral-up-transform}
 \int_{-1}^1e^{tx}\upf(x)\dd x=\mathcal U(t),
 \qquad
 \int_{-1}^1e^{i\omega x}\upf(x)\dd x=\phi(\omega).
\end{equation}

\subsection{Stieltjes transforms and all resolvent powers}

For \(z\in\C\setminus[0,1]\), define
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:GY-def}
 G_Y(z)=\E\frac1{z-Y}
 =\int_0^1\frac{\dd v}{z-\Q(v)}.
\end{equation}
For \(|z|>1\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:GY-moment-series}
 G_Y(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{m_n}{z^{n+1}}.
\end{equation}
Similarly, for \(z\notin[-1,1]\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:GZ-def}
 G_Z(z)=\int_{-1}^1\frac{\upf(x)}{z-x}\dd x
 =\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_n}{z^{n+1}},
 \qquad |z|>1.
\end{equation}

Applying \cref{p10:eq:analytic-kernel-F} to a resolvent primitive gives all higher powers.
For every integer \(n\ge1\),
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1\frac{\F(x)}{(z-x)^{n+1}}\dd x
 &=\frac1n\left[\frac1{(z-1)^n}-\E\frac1{(z-Y)^n}\right],
 \label{p10:eq:resolvent-F-n}\\
 \int_0^1\frac{1-\F(x)}{(z-x)^{n+1}}\dd x
 &=\frac1n\left[\E\frac1{(z-Y)^n}-\frac1{z^n}\right].
 \label{p10:eq:resolvent-up-n}
\end{align}
For \(n=1\),
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1\frac{\F(x)}{(z-x)^2}\dd x
 &=\frac1{z-1}-G_Y(z),\label{p10:eq:resolvent-F-2}\\
 \int_0^1\frac{1-\F(x)}{(z-x)^2}\dd x
 &=G_Y(z)-\frac1z.\label{p10:eq:resolvent-up-2}
\end{align}
Using the logarithmic primitive, with a branch analytic off \([0,1]\),
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1\frac{\F(x)}{z-x}\dd x
 &=\E\log(z-Y)-\log(z-1),
 \label{p10:eq:log-resolvent-F}\\
 \int_0^1\frac{1-\F(x)}{z-x}\dd x
 &=\log z-\E\log(z-Y).
 \label{p10:eq:log-resolvent-up}
\end{align}
These formulas furnish analytic continuations and boundary-value formulas for Hilbert
transforms by approaching the cut from above and below.

\subsection{A transform fixed point and the full resolvent hierarchy}
\label{p10:subsec:resolvent-fixed-point}

The affine random fixed point for the centered up law produces more than a moment
series.  Let
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Rn-def}
 R_n(z)=\int_{-1}^1\frac{\upf(t)}{(z-t)^n}\dd t,
 \qquad z\in\C\setminus[-1,1],\quad n\ge1.
\end{equation}
Thus \(R_1=G_Z\).  If \(X\) has density \(\upf\), then
\(X\overset d=(X'+U)/2\), where \(U\) is uniform on \([-1,1]\) and independent of
\(X'\overset d=X\).

\begin{theorem}[logarithmic fixed point and higher resolvents]
\label{p10:thm:Cauchy-fixed-hierarchy}
With the logarithm branch normalized at infinity,
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Cauchy-log-fixed}
 \boxed{
 R_1(z)=\E\log\!\left(\frac{2z-X+1}{2z-X-1}\right).}
\end{equation}
It follows that
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Cauchy-diffeq-new}
 R_1'(z)=2\bigl(R_1(2z+1)-R_1(2z-1)\bigr),
\end{equation}
and, for every \(n\ge1\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:resolvent-hierarchy-up}
 \boxed{
 R_{n+1}(z)
 =\frac{2^n}{n}\bigl(R_n(2z-1)-R_n(2z+1)\bigr).}
\end{equation}
For the inverse-Fabius resolvents
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Sn-def}
 S_n(z)=\int_0^1\frac{\dd v}{(z-\Q(v))^n},
 \qquad z\in\C\setminus[0,1],
\end{equation}
we have
\begin{align}
 S_n(z)&=2^nR_n(2z-1),
 \label{p10:eq:S-R-affine}\\
 S_{n+1}(z)
 &=\frac{2^{n+1}}n\bigl(S_n(2z-1)-S_n(2z)\bigr).
 \label{p10:eq:resolvent-hierarchy-Q}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Condition on \(X'\) and integrate the uniform variable:
\[
 \E\left[\left.\frac1{z-(X'+U)/2}\right|X'\right]
 =\int_{-1}^1\frac{\dd u}{2z-X'-u}
 =\log\frac{2z-X'+1}{2z-X'-1}.
\]
This proves \eqref{p10:eq:Cauchy-log-fixed}.  Differentiation gives
\eqref{p10:eq:Cauchy-diffeq-new}.  Since
\(
 R_n(z)=(-1)^{n-1}R_1^{(n-1)}(z)/(n-1)!
\), differentiating \eqref{p10:eq:Cauchy-diffeq-new} repeatedly gives
\eqref{p10:eq:resolvent-hierarchy-up}.  Finally, \(\Q(V)\) with
\(V\sim\mathrm{Unif}[0,1]\) has the same law as \(Y=(X+1)/2\), which gives
\eqref{p10:eq:S-R-affine}; substitution into \eqref{p10:eq:resolvent-hierarchy-up} gives
\eqref{p10:eq:resolvent-hierarchy-Q}.
\end{proof}


\subsection{Entire-kernel calculus}

If \(h(x)=\sum_{n\ge0}a_nx^n\) is entire and the series is dominated on the compact
support, then
\begin{align}
 \int_{-1}^1h(x)\upf(x)\dd x
 &=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_n\mu_n,
 \label{p10:eq:entire-kernel-up}\\
 \int_0^1h(x)\dd\F(x)
 &=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_nm_n.
 \label{p10:eq:entire-kernel-dF}
\end{align}
For instance,
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Bessel-up}
 \int_{-1}^1J_0(tx)\upf(x)\dd x
 =\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}
 \frac{(-1)^n(t^2/4)^n}{(n!)^2}\mu_{2n}.
\end{equation}
Together with \cref{p10:eq:analytic-kernel-F}, this gives convergent moment series for
Bessel, Airy, hypergeometric, and polylogarithmic kernels without requiring a separate
quadrature of the flat endpoint function.

\section{Products, order statistics, and correlation integrals}

Integrals containing products of \(\F\) and \(\upf\) are generally not rational, but
they have exact probabilistic meanings that lead to useful transform formulas and
asymptotics.

\subsection{Products of distribution and survival powers}

Let \(Y_1,\ldots,Y_{m+n}\) be independent copies of \(Y\), and define
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Amax-Bmin}
 A_m=\max(Y_1,\ldots,Y_m),
 \qquad
 B_n=\min(Y_{m+1},\ldots,Y_{m+n}).
\end{equation}
The two blocks are independent and
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:gap-event}
 \Prob(A_m\le x<B_n)=\F(x)^m(1-\F(x))^n.
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[weighted order-statistic gap]\label{p10:thm:order-gap}
For integers \(m,n\ge1\) and \(\Re p>-1\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:weighted-gap}
 \int_0^1x^p\F(x)^m(1-\F(x))^n\dd x
 =\frac1{p+1}\E\left[
 \bigl(B_n^{p+1}-A_m^{p+1}\bigr)\ind_{\{A_m<B_n\}}
 \right].
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:gap-unweighted}
 \int_0^1\F(x)^m(1-\F(x))^n\dd x
 =\E(B_n-A_m)_+.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Insert the indicator of the event in \cref{p10:eq:gap-event}, integrate, and apply Fubini.
For each realization, the allowed interval is \([A_m,B_n)\) if \(A_m<B_n\), and empty
otherwise.  Its \(x^p\)-integral is the right side of \cref{p10:eq:weighted-gap}.
\end{proof}

Since \(\upf(x)=1-\F(x)\) on \([0,1]\), this theorem covers all integer products
\(\F^m\upf^n\) on the positive half of the support.  Two useful special cases are
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Fm-order}
 \int_0^1\F(x)^m\dd x=\E(1-A_m)=\E\min(Y_1,\ldots,Y_m),
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:F2-gini-relation}
 \int_0^1\F(x)^2\dd x
 =\frac12-\int_0^1\F(x)(1-\F(x))\dd x.
\end{equation}

When the density factor is present, substitution gives a closed beta value for arbitrary
complex exponents in their convergence domain:
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:density-beta}
 \int_0^1\F(x)^a(1-\F(x))^b\F'(x)\dd x
 =B(a+1,b+1).
\end{equation}
Using \(\F'(x)=2\upf(2x-1)\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:up-density-beta}
 2\int_0^1\F(x)^a(1-\F(x))^b\upf(2x-1)\dd x
 =B(a+1,b+1).
\end{equation}

\subsection{Quantiles and ordinary order statistics}

If \(Y_{r:n}\) is the \(r\)-th order statistic of \(n\) independent Fabius variables,
then \(Y_{r:n}=\Q(V_{r:n})\) in distribution, where \(V_{r:n}\) has beta density.
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:quantile-order-stat}
 \E Y_{r:n}^p
 =\frac1{B(r,n-r+1)}
 \int_0^1v^{r-1}(1-v)^{n-r}\Q(v)^p\dd v.
\end{equation}
Symmetry gives
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:order-stat-symmetry}
 \E Y_{r:n}^p
 =\E(1-Y_{n+1-r:n})^p,
 \qquad
 \E Y_{r:n}+\E Y_{n+1-r:n}=1.
\end{equation}
Expanding the beta polynomial in \cref{p10:eq:quantile-order-stat} reduces every integer
\(n,r\) to a finite combination of the mixed quantile integrals appearing in
\cref{p10:eq:power-complete}.

\subsection{The Gini integral and the sinc product}

For two independent copies \(Y,Y'\), the standard distribution identity is
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Gini-prob}
 \int_0^1\F(x)(1-\F(x))\dd x
 =\frac12\E|Y-Y'|.
\end{equation}
The difference is \((Z-Z')/2\), and its characteristic function is
\(\phi(t/2)^2\).  The absolute-moment formula
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:absolute-char}
 \E|X|=\frac2\pi\int_0^\infty
 \frac{1-\Re\varphi_X(t)}{t^2}\dd t
\end{equation}
therefore yields an integral containing only the infinite sinc product.

\begin{theorem}[Gini--sinc identity]\label{p10:thm:gini-sinc}
With \(\phi\) as in \cref{p10:eq:phi-product},
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Gini-sinc}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \int_0^1\F(x)(1-\F(x))\dd x
 =\frac1{2\pi}\int_0^\infty
 \frac{1-\phi(t)^2}{t^2}\dd t.}
\end{equation}
Numerically,
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Gini-numeric}
 \int_0^1\F(x)(1-\F(x))\dd x
 =0.0955632320160066\ldots,
\end{equation}
so
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:F2-numeric}
 \int_0^1\F(x)^2\dd x
 =0.4044367679839934\ldots.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

This is a direct link between a nonlinear real-space integral of \(\F\) and the square
of the Rvachev infinite product.  It does not appear to simplify to the rational moment
constants.

\subsection{Autocorrelation and \(L^2\) energy}

Define
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Cautocorr}
 \mathcal C(a)=\int_\R\upf(x)\upf(x+a)\dd x.
\end{equation}
Since \(\upf\) is even, the Wiener--Khinchin formula gives
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:autocorr-Fourier}
 \mathcal C(a)=\frac1{2\pi}
 \int_\R e^{-ita}\phi(t)^2\dd t.
\end{equation}
At \(a=0\), Parseval's identity gives
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:up-L2}
 \int_\R\upf(x)^2\dd x
 =\frac1\pi\int_0^\infty\phi(t)^2\dd t
 =0.808873535967971\ldots.
\end{equation}
More generally, derivatives of \(\mathcal C\) at the origin are convergent spectral
moments whenever the corresponding weighted sinc-product integral converges; the fast
decay results developed elsewhere in the repository provide quantitative truncation
bounds.

\section{Additional integral-transform identities}

\subsection{A transform relation for inverse resolvents}

The general identity \cref{p10:thm:AB} can be applied in the \(v\)-variable as well.  Taking
\(A(x)=x\) and
\(B(v)=\log(z/(z-v))\) gives
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:inverse-log-resolvent}
 \int_0^1\frac{\Q(v)}{z-v}\dd v
 +\int_0^1\log\frac{z}{z-\F(x)}\dd x
 =\log\frac{z}{z-1}.
\end{equation}
With \(B(v)=(z-v)^{-1}\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:inverse-square-resolvent}
 \int_0^1\frac{\Q(v)}{(z-v)^2}\dd v
 +\int_0^1\frac{\dd x}{z-\F(x)}
 =\frac1{z-1}.
\end{equation}
These are nonlinear resolvent dualities: a transform of the quantile is paired with a
composition transform of the distribution function.

\subsection{Frullani representations for logarithmic constants}

For \(0<Y\le1\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Frullani-log}
 -\log Y=\int_0^\infty\frac{e^{-tY}-e^{-t}}t\dd t.
\end{equation}
Averaging and using \(P(-t)\) gives
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:log-Frullani-product}
 \int_0^1\frac{\F(x)}x\dd x
 =\int_0^\infty\frac{P(-t)-e^{-t}}t\dd t,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Pminus-product}
 P(-t)=\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}
 \frac{1-e^{-t/2^k}}{t/2^k}.
\end{equation}
For \(a>0\), the negative moment itself has the Mellin--Laplace representation
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:negative-Mellin-Laplace}
 \MPartiii(-a)=\frac1{\Gamma(a)}\int_0^\infty t^{a-1}P(-t)\dd t.
\end{equation}
This is an independent numerical route to \cref{p10:eq:negative-moment-rational-series} and
connects the negative-moment constant \(C_*\) to the dyadic product on the positive
Laplace axis.

\subsection{A smoothing difference equation for \texorpdfstring{\(\MPartiii\)}{M}}

Let
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Ndef}
 N(s)=\E(1+Y)^s
 =\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom skm_k.
\end{equation}
The series is entire because of \cref{p10:lem:moment-decay}.  Integrating the uniform
variable in \cref{p10:eq:smoothing} with an arbitrary complex exponent gives the entire
difference relation
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:M-difference}
 (s+1)2^s\MPartiii(s)=N(s+1)-\MPartiii(s+1),
\end{equation}
with the value at \(s=-1\) interpreted by continuity.  At nonnegative integer \(s\),
coefficient comparison recovers \cref{p10:eq:moment-recurrence}; at negative \(s\), it is
equivalent to \cref{p10:eq:negative-recurrence}.  Thus the positive rational moments and
the superexponentially growing negative moments are two sides of one entire
difference equation.

\section{Numerical experiments and reproducibility}
\label{p10:sec:numerics-new}\label{p10:sec:numerics}

The accompanying program \texttt{fractional\_frontier\_experiments.py} is fully
commented and deterministic.  It reconstructs \(\upf\) by FFT inversion of the sinc
product on a period-four grid with \(2^{18}\) points; the support \([-1,1]\) prevents
periodic overlap.  The Fabius function is then obtained from
\(\F(x)=\upf(x-1)\) on \([0,1]\), and a monotone inverse gives \(\Q\).
No Monte Carlo sampling or network access is used.

The program independently checks four parts of the new analysis:
\begin{enumerate}
\item the exterior series \eqref{p10:eq:up-fraclap-exterior} and
\eqref{p10:eq:up-Riesz-exterior} against direct quadrature of their defining kernels;
\item the logarithmic Cauchy fixed point \eqref{p10:eq:Cauchy-log-fixed}, differential
equation \eqref{p10:eq:Cauchy-diffeq-new}, and hierarchy
\eqref{p10:eq:resolvent-hierarchy-up};
\item the normalized endpoint ratio in
\eqref{p10:eq:Q-frac-primitive-eta} and its first digamma correction;
\item the normalized fractional derivative in
\eqref{p10:eq:Q-frac-derivative-eta}.  The derivative is obtained by a symmetric
five-point differentiation of \(I^{1-\sigma}\Q\), avoiding the severe conditioning
loss in direct division by the flat density \(\F'\).
\end{enumerate}

\input{assets/Fabius_Fractional_Integral_Transform_Frontiers/new_numerical_results.tex}

At \(|x|=1.2\), where the exterior moment series converges most slowly among the
displayed points, eighteen exact moments already give errors between
\(10^{-8}\) and \(4\times10^{-7}\) for the fractional Laplacian; at \(|x|=2\),
the discrepancy is at floating-point roundoff.  The Cauchy-transform identities have
residuals below \(10^{-16}\).  In the endpoint tables, the first digamma correction
reduces the primitive error from about \(10^{-1}\) to about \(2\times10^{-2}\), and
the fractional-derivative error from about \(1.4\times10^{-1}\) to about
\(1.5\times10^{-2}\) at the smallest resolved \(y\).  The trend is consistent with
the predicted \(O((\log T)/T)\) remainder; FFT resolution, rather than the asymptotic
formula, becomes the limiting factor farther into the flat endpoint.

The script writes a human-readable ledger \path{numerical_results.txt}, a JSON file
\path{numerical_results.json}, and the LaTeX tables included above.  Running
\begin{verbatim}
python fractional_frontier_experiments.py
\end{verbatim}
regenerates all three files.


\section{Conjectures and frontier directions}

\subsection{Critical fractional Sobolev regularity of the inverse}

\begin{conjecture}[sharp fractional Sobolev boundary]\label{p10:conj:Q-Wsp}
For \(0<s<1\), \(1\le p<\infty\), and \(\beta>0\),
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Q-Wsp-conj}
 \Q^\beta\in W^{s,p}(0,1)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad sp\le1.
\end{equation}
At the critical line \(sp=1\), membership should be strong rather than merely weak,
with the stretched-exponential inverse endpoint factor providing the additional
integrability.  The one-sided derivative theorem
\cref{p10:thm:Q-fractional-derivative-Lp} proves the corresponding statement in the
Riemann--Liouville endpoint scale.  A proof of \eqref{p10:eq:Q-Wsp-conj} should combine a
fractional Hardy equivalence with a two-sided localization and the repository's
all-orders inverse expansion.
\end{conjecture}

\subsection{Function-space realization of the Floquet corrector}

The symbol \(m_\alpha(s)\) in \eqref{p10:eq:known-fractional-defect-new} is bounded above
and below on the positive axis for each fixed real \(\alpha\), because \(\Pi\) is
positive and periodic.  It is therefore natural to expect a bounded causal operator on
weighted Laplace--Hardy or Mellin--Sobolev spaces.

\begin{conjecture}[dyadic fractional calculus]\label{p10:conj:Floquet-space}
There is a natural Banach scale \(\mathcal X_\rho\) containing the global signed Fabius
extension such that:
\begin{enumerate}
\item every \(\mathcal C_\alpha\) extends boundedly and invertibly to
\(\mathcal X_\rho\);
\item the scale-cocycle identity \eqref{p10:eq:Floquet-cocycle} becomes an operator cocycle
under dyadic dilation;
\item \(\mathcal C_\alpha\mathbf D^\alpha\) is the unique continuous-order extension of
the integer derivative orbit satisfying the cocycle and a mild spectral boundedness
condition.
\end{enumerate}
The Mellin variable \(\log_2s\) turns the problem into periodic Floquet theory, while
the nonzero Fourier modes of \(\log\Pi\) should control norm distortion.
\end{conjecture}

\subsection{Arithmetic nonlocal tails}

For rational \(s\) or \(a\), the series in
\cref{p10:thm:up-fraclap-exterior,p10:thm:up-Riesz-exterior} has rational moment coefficients
multiplied by explicit gamma constants.  The arithmetic nature of special values such
as
\(
 (-\Delta)^{1/2}\upf(2)
\)
and \((\mathcal I_{1/2}\upf)(2)\) is unknown.

\begin{conjecture}[noncollapse of exterior nonlocal values]
\label{p10:conj:nonlocal-arithmetic}
Except for identities forced by gamma reflection and the rational moment recurrence,
the special values of the exterior fractional-Laplacian and Riesz-potential series at
rational \(|x|>1\) do not reduce to finite combinations of algebraic numbers and powers
of \(\pi\).  A first tractable target is linear independence of several values over the
field generated by the relevant gamma constants.
\end{conjecture}



\subsection{Near-rigidity of the negative-moment phase}

\begin{conjecture}[phase rigidity]\label{p10:conj:phase-constant}
The continuous one-periodic limiting profile \(C(\theta)\) in
\cref{p10:eq:Ctheta-product} is constant:
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:Ctheta-conjecture}
 C(\theta)\equiv C_*.
\end{equation}
\end{conjecture}

A weaker and perhaps more realistic alternative is that \(C\) is nonconstant but its
first Fourier harmonics are suppressed by the same spectral mechanism responsible for
the extremely small periodic oscillations in the endpoint asymptotics studied elsewhere
in the repository.  The exact recurrence reduces this question to the Fourier analysis
of the periodic product \cref{p10:eq:Ctheta-product}.  A rigorous high-precision
test should compute \(A(\theta)\) independently by \cref{p10:eq:negative-Mellin-Laplace},
rather than by the same moment data used in the recurrence.

\subsection{Completely regular growth of the entire Mellin function}

The maximum modulus is known exactly through \cref{p10:eq:maxmod-exact}, but directional
cancellation remains open.

\begin{conjecture}[indicator function]\label{p10:conj:indicator}
The entire function \(\MPartiii\) has completely regular growth of order \(2\) with indicator
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:indicator-conjecture}
 h(\vartheta)=\frac{\log2}{2}
 \max\{0,-\cos\vartheta\}^2.
\end{equation}
\end{conjecture}

The upper bound follows immediately from
\(|\MPartiii(re^{i\vartheta})|\le\MPartiii(r\max(0,-\cos\vartheta))\).  The difficult part is a
matching lower bound away from the negative real axis, where oscillation in
\(e^{i\Im(s)\log Y}\) may create zeros and exceptional directions.

\subsection{Arithmetic nature of the new constants}

The quantities
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:new-constants}
 C_*,\qquad \MPartiii(-r),\qquad -\E\log Y,
 \qquad \int_0^1\F(1-\F),\qquad \int_\R\upf^2
\end{equation}
are defined by positive rational series or infinite sinc products, but no reduction to
standard constants is visible.

\begin{conjecture}\label{p10:conj:irrationality}
The constants \(C_*\), \(\MPartiii(-r)\) for every positive integer \(r\), and
\(-\E\log Y\) are irrational; at least one of the stronger properties of
transcendence or algebraic independence should hold among them.
\end{conjecture}

This is deliberately stated as a research target, not as numerical evidence for a
specific transcendence theorem.  Possible tools include Mahler-type functional
equations for \(P(t)\), arithmetic estimates for the rational Newton partial sums, and
Pad\'e approximants controlled by the moment recurrence.

\subsection{Complete monotonicity of normalized errors}

The sequence \(C_r\) is decreasing by \cref{p10:eq:Cr-rec}.  Numerics suggest much more.

\begin{conjecture}\label{p10:conj:complete-monotone}
The error sequence \(C_r-C_*\) is completely monotone:
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:complete-monotone-conj}
 (-1)^k\Delta^k(C_r-C_*)\ge0
 \qquad(r\ge1,\ k\ge0).
\end{equation}
\end{conjecture}

A proof might represent \(C_r-C_*\) as a Hausdorff moment sequence derived from the
positive tail product \(\prod_{j\ge r}(1-\rho(j))\).

\subsection{Order-statistic endpoint regimes}

When the sample size \(n\) grows, the minimum \(Y_{1:n}\) probes precisely the
Lambert--\(W\) endpoint regime.  Since
\[
 \Prob(Y_{1:n}>x)=(1-\F(x))^n,
\]
its moments are
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:min-order-moment}
 \E Y_{1:n}^p=p\int_0^1x^{p-1}(1-\F(x))^n\dd x.
\end{equation}
A promising direction is a uniform saddle-point expansion in \(p\) and \(n\), with the
periodic endpoint phase transported into order-statistic oscillations.  The corresponding
maximum follows by symmetry.  This would connect the present integral calculus directly
to the repository's inverse-Lambert and half-integer spectral programs.

\subsection{General base and \texorpdfstring{\(q\)}{q}-deformations}

Replace the dyadic weights by \(b^{-k}\), normalized so the support remains \([0,1]\).
The distributional smoothing, inverse-pair calculus, and fractional identities survive
unchanged, while the product \(P(t)\), Bernoulli cumulants, and negative-moment
normalization acquire base \(b\).  The expected leading law is
\begin{equation}\label{p10:eq:baseb-heuristic}
 \E Y_b^{-r}
 \asymp C_b\frac{b^{r(r+1)/2}}{\Gamma(r)}.
\end{equation}
Determining \(C_b\), its phase profile, and its relationship with \(q=1/b\) binomial
coefficients is a natural extension.

\subsection{Formalization roadmap}

The most accessible Lean targets are:
\begin{enumerate}
\item package the remaining incomplete, arbitrary-absolutely-continuous scope of
      \cref{p10:thm:AB}; measure-level quantile transport and the full-interval
      explicit-primitive and pointwise-\(C^1\) layer-cake core are already formal;
\item the power identity \cref{p10:eq:power-complete} and its polynomial corollaries;
\item the positive and negative moment recurrences;
\item positivity, monotonicity, and convergence of \(C_r\);
\item the order-statistic gap identity;
\item finite truncations and certified tail bounds for the Newton and sinc-product
series.
\end{enumerate}
The entire-function and fractional-integral portions require more analytic infrastructure,
but can be decomposed into dominated-convergence, holomorphic-parameter integration,
and beta-integral lemmas.

\section{Summary of principal formulas}

For convenient reference, the central formulas are collected here.

\begin{resultbox}[Distribution and transforms]
\begin{align*}
 Y&=\sum_{k\ge1}2^{-k}U_k,\qquad Z=2Y-1,\\
 P(t)&=\prod_{k\ge1}\frac{e^{t/2^k}-1}{t/2^k},\qquad
 \phi(t)=\prod_{k\ge1}\frac{\sin(t/2^k)}{t/2^k},\\
 \int_0^1e^{tx}\F(x)\dd x&=\frac{e^t-P(t)}t,\qquad
 \int_0^1e^{tx}(1-\F(x))\dd x=\frac{P(t)-1}t.
\end{align*}
\end{resultbox}

\begin{resultbox}[Inverse-pair integration]
\begin{align*}
 A(z)B(\F(z))-A(0)B(0)
 &=\int_0^zA'B\circ\F
 +\int_0^{\F(z)}A\circ\Q\,B',\\
 p\int_0^1x^{p-1}\F(x)^q\dd x
 +q\int_0^1v^{q-1}\Q(v)^p\dd v&=1,\\
 s\int_0^1e^{sx+t\F(x)}\dd x
 +t\int_0^1e^{tv+s\Q(v)}\dd v&=e^{s+t}-1.
\end{align*}
\end{resultbox}

\begin{resultbox}[Entire moment calculus]
\begin{align*}
 \MPartiii(s)&=\int_0^1\Q(v)^s\dd v,\\
 \MPartiii(s)&=\sum_{k\ge0}(-1)^k\binom skm_k
 =2^{-s}\sum_{j\ge0}\binom{s}{2j}\mu_{2j},\\
 \int_0^1x^{s-1}\F(x)\dd x&=\frac{1-\MPartiii(s)}s,\qquad
 \int_0^1x^{s-1}\upf(x)\dd x=\frac{\MPartiii(s)}s.
\end{align*}
\end{resultbox}

\begin{resultbox}[Negative moments and fractional primitives]
\begin{align*}
 A(s+1)&=\frac{2^{s+1}}s(A(s)-D(s)),\\
 \MPartiii(-r)&\sim C_*\frac{2^{r(r+1)/2}}{\Gamma(r)},\\
 I_0^\alpha\F^q(x)
 &=\frac{q}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}
 \int_0^{\F(x)}(x-\Q(v))^\alpha v^{q-1}\dd v,\\
 I_0^\alpha\Q^p(v)
 &=\frac{p}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}
 \int_0^{\Q(v)}(v-\F(x))^\alpha x^{p-1}\dd x.
\end{align*}
\end{resultbox}

\begin{resultbox}[New fractional endpoint laws]
For \(T=\log(1/y)\), \(\eta=y\Q'/\Q\sim\sqrt{\log2/(2T)}\),
\begin{align*}
 I_{0+}^{a}(\Q^\beta)(y)
 &=\frac{y^a\Q(y)^\beta}{\Gamma(a+1)}
   \bigl(1-\beta H_a\eta+o(\eta)\bigr),\\
 D_{0+}^{\sigma}(\Q^\beta)(y)
 &=\frac{y^{-\sigma}\Q(y)^\beta}{\Gamma(1-\sigma)}
   \bigl(1+\beta K_\sigma\eta+o(\eta)\bigr),\\
 D_{0+}^{\sigma}(\Q^\beta)\in L^p(0,\tfrac12)
 &\Longleftrightarrow \sigma p\le1.
\end{align*}
\end{resultbox}

\begin{resultbox}[New nonlocal and resolvent formulas]
For \(|x|>1\),
\begin{align*}
 (-\Delta)^s\upf(x)
 &=-c_{1,s}|x|^{-1-2s}
   \sum_{j\ge0}\frac{(1+2s)_{2j}}{(2j)!}
   \frac{\mu_{2j}}{|x|^{2j}},\\
 (\mathcal I_a\upf)(x)
 &=d_{1,a}|x|^{a-1}
   \sum_{j\ge0}\frac{(1-a)_{2j}}{(2j)!}
   \frac{\mu_{2j}}{|x|^{2j}},\\
 R_{n+1}(z)
 &=\frac{2^n}{n}\bigl(R_n(2z-1)-R_n(2z+1)\bigr).
\end{align*}
The fractional self-differential orbit is restored exactly by the log-periodic multiplier
\(
 m_\alpha(s)=\Pi(\log_2s-\alpha)/\Pi(\log_2s)
\), which obeys a dyadic scale-cocycle law.
\end{resultbox}


\section{Conclusion}

The probability/quantile viewpoint converts the integral theory of the Fabius--Rvachev
system into a coherent calculus rather than a list of isolated evaluations.  The
inverse-pair identity organizes indefinite weighted integrals; the entire moment function
organizes fractional and negative powers; Riemann--Liouville operators organize repeated
antiderivatives; and the infinite sinc product organizes correlation and fractional
Sobolev energies.  The inverse endpoint has a particularly clean continuous-order law:
digamma coefficients interpolate the harmonic-number corrections, and the line
\(\sigma p=1\) is the exact strong integrability boundary.  Nonlocal differentiation
creates algebraic tails whose complete coefficient sequence remains rational before the
universal gamma normalization.  The negative-moment recurrence is also especially
useful: it extracts an additional constant and an analytic periodic phase directly from
the dyadic smoothing law, with exponentially fast normalization.

The next decisive steps are to determine whether the phase profile is exactly constant,
to connect its Fourier modes with the spectral corrections already present in endpoint
asymptotics, and to formalize the remaining incomplete arbitrary-absolutely-continuous
inverse-pair scope and the moment recurrences.  These directions
are sufficiently concrete to support both high-precision experiments and Lean proofs.
